CHAPTER V.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Academy.—From a popular point of view, this year's Academy is distinctly bright and interesting, not less so because the critics have been unable to dis- cover so many masterpieces of the "pictures of the year" as usual. "A Venetian Funeral' by Mr. Frank Brangwyn is by general consent one of the best pictures in the exhibition, and another of exceptional interest is Mr. • Abbey's "Columbus in the New World." The interest which was centred in last year'*" The Cheat," is once again aroused by the Hon. John Collier in his "Indeed, indeed, repentance, oft. I swore," which is sufficiently nebulous to arouse curiosity, though the subject representing a lady on the sofa in the firelight is not L 3 dramatic as that of lest year. It is the imaginative pictures which draw the largest crowds, though it does not follow that they are the best. Amon.' the less pretentious works is a simple flower study by Princess Patricia of Connaught, which has been given a place in this year's Academy. The Marchioness of Granby too, has some sketches, and the catalogue also includes the names of several other dis- tinguished amateur artists.

The Portraits.—The Royal portraits in the Academy include two large pictures of the King and Queen, painted by Mr. Colin Forbes for the Canadian Houses of Parliament, and there is also a charming portrait of Princess Gustavus Adolphus by Mr. .1. J. Shannon, representing Princess Margaret seated, and amply dressed in white. Very striking too is Mr. Dick. se's full-length portrait of the Duchess of Westminster, whilst Mr. Sargeent's most interesting exhibit is his portrait of" Maud, daughter of Mr. George Coats, Esq." A portrait group by Mr. Sholto Douglas, is notable as including a full-size picture of a motor-car, rather a novel feature in the exhibits at Burlington House. The large manber of portraits at the Academy , shows how much our artists are engaged in painting on commission, which is a pity, as such productions are , not of much interest as a rule, except to the originals and their friends. They are mostly portraits of women , and girls, and one sees them in the opening day of the Academy, explaining to groups of their friends how the ' painting scarcely does them justice. Dress at Burlington House.--The prevalence of the : -ostrich feather, whether in the form of boas, or waving from the latest millinery trin-npbs, must have struck even the most casual observer of dress, at the private view. The women who boasted no sign of an ostrich feather in some form or other, might almost have been counted on the fingers of one hand, Another notable feature was the short glace coat almost covered with ' strapping, tuckings, and frills. Taken as a whole, the i millinery was more curious than pretty, pc .cock's feathers, and alas, the paradise plume, waving wilding about at considerable heights, while a not uncommon spectacle was that of flowers, fruit and feathers, all I piled together on one hat or toque. The impossible angles too, at which the majority of the " cr; Along " I were worn, filled one with amazement, and also specula- I tion as to how long it had taken patient maids to build I up a foundation of hair, real or acquired," or both, upon which to poise them.' The favourite colours appeared to be blues, heliotropes, and lavenders, in I every conceivable shade. The Eel-Skin Robe.—Sheath-dresses are rapidly coming to the front for evening wear, a fact women I with a good figure and carriage will appreciate. We have tightly-fitting and clinging robes,covered entirely I with jet, or steel, " raven's wing "spangles, s; ed pearl , embroideries, or again, little discs of mother-of-pearl, set in silver einoroidery—but undoubtedly the best , effects are obtained in the black or "raven's wing " I specimens. And George Gretton, whose sense of humour alone saved him from extinction, big man as he ass, tapped at his daughter's door. "Your mother w,ll not dine to-night, Madge; but I shall be ready in ten minutes. Hurry up, there's a dear." And when dinner was over, he said:

"Be a good girl, Madge. And, remember I'm supposed to have spoken to you very seriously---very seriously, indeed." (To be continuea.)

" AFRAID TO EAT."

'Bile Beans Banish a Farmer's Terrible

Stomach Troubles.

Glassel is a picturesque spot on the Deeside, a few Miles from Balmoral Castle, the Scottish home of our Ring and Queen. At Greeburn, Glassel, lives Mr. Archibald Copland, a farmer, who has recently been cured of severe stomach disorders by Bile Beans. Relating his story to an Aberdeen reporter,Mr. Copland Eaid :--` For years I was a terrible sufferer from indigestion and biliousness. I endured such frightful Pain after taking food that Lbecame absolutely afraid to eat. I felt dull and sick, my stomach was sore, and the sharp, stinging pains in the stomach spread to my Shoulder blades, racking me as though I was being torn t° Pieces. lhat awful feeling-continued until I was quite unfit to work. I couldn't sleep, and became so Worn out that death seemed to stare me in the face. "The doctors told me that the lining was worn off 157 stomach, and I would have to live on boiled milk and very light soups. But all the special treatments 112 WI all the ctoctor's mixtures only eased the pains for a isle. At last I tried Bile Beans, and after taking the third dose felt an almost indescribable relief. Before had finished two boxes I could eat anything that the Other members of the family took. lam now as well in health as ever I have been." Bile Beans are an unfailing remedy for all liver, " Stomach, and bowel disorders. All chemists stock them at Is. Ed. and 2s. 98. per box: or they may be obtained at the same prices from the Bile Bean Manufacturing Co., Red Cross Street, London.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

HEIRESS TO A GREAT Aux's EGO.

ITo be born heir to wealth beyond the dreams lof avarice is too common a happening nowadays to provoke comment, but to find oneself on arrival in this plane of existence the presumptive heiress to a great auk's egg, among other things, is an event quite out of the ordinary. Such, however, says Vanity Fair, is the case with the little lady who made her first appearance lately at Drayton House, in Norfolk. But those who know say that an even more priceless inheritance will be the extraordinary affection felt by all classes residing in the King's County for the small heiress's grandparents, Mr. and Mrs. John Walter. THE NEW BOLERO. The perennially popular bolero, still as great a. ' favourite as ever, seems to be modifying and developing in quite a novel fashion during this spring of 1906. The sleeves have developed into cape-like affairs, which hang loosely from the shoulder and barely reach the elbow; while the body of bolero has formed itself into something resembling rather a smart wrap than a tight- fitting coat. An excellent example of this new 1 bolero is illustrated in our sketch. It is fashioned in some smooth, thin, woollen material—fine

light cloth or serge—and is extremely simple in form. The back of the bolero fits closely tio the figure, but the fronts, Ming over the shoulders, hang loosely from the bust. A turn-down collar of velvet z.nd three large, handsome buttons on each side of the front form its only adorn- ments. The sleeves are quite loose and cape- like, being cut in two pieces, which are buttoned together up the front with three large buttons.

WHAT °IS WORN.

Petticoats of muslin flounced over silk will be ornamented with embroidery or lace and bows, or loops of ribbon set at intervals. Coarse net flounces accordion-pleated with endless rows of ribbon are another fashion in petticoats. The white flounces have ribbons the colour of the silk petticoat itself. A Pompadour silk petticoat will have the design carried out in tiny coloured bebe ribbons to match the various colours. Black and white petticoats will be worn and tar- tan is still favoured.

Elaboration in hairdressing is a noticeable feature just now, says the Woman at Home. Jewelled combs and high Spanish combs beauti- fully carved are worn. Flowers and tinsel tulle form the turban-like head-dresses. Matrons will affect wreaths of grapes or berries of different kinds; clumps of roses, 'flowers, and ribbon are all 'figuring in our new coiffure. Por the Empire dresses we have, the areek bandelettes. which can be made with the ordin- ary tinsel ribbon. Small coronets of gold-leaved maidenhair, French ferns, or rose-leaves can be made by treating the ordinary artificial foliage with a little gold paint. I saw a charming wreath made of maidenhair fern which had thus been painted by a clever woman with luminous green paint It rested on a twist of emerald tulle, and made a most charming and inexpen- sive coronet. Jewelled laoo tiaras have to be carefully wired.

GIRL'S SCHOOL FROCK.

Our sketch shews a usefUl school frock for a girl. The blouse being quite separate, the frock could be worn throughout the term with a change of thick or cooler blouses. The skirt should be of a light-weight grey tweed. It is cut in the corselet style, and supported with straps of tweed cver the shoulders. These should be crossed at the back to prevent them slipping off

the shoulders. The hem is simply trimmed with two wide, stitched cros.sway straps of the mate- rial. The blo ase accompanying this skirt is of silky grey linen, the fulness 'arranged in large tucks about the yoke, and at the neck there is a rrettily-qhaped collar embroidered in some dainty coloured silk. The sleeves are very full, and finished with cuffs to match the collar. In the sketch the sleeves are depicted reaching to just below the elbow, but if preferred these could easily be extended to the wrist. THE MOST FAVOURED JEWELS. - -

- Emeralds lead the way as the most fashionable of all precious stones this season, and "the more expensive they get the greater seems the demand for them," is the verdict of a well-known jeweller. Every month their value is enhanced, and the wonderful collections owned by Lady Ludlow, Lady Londesborough, and Lady Helen Stavordale must be of untold value. Her Majesty the Queen is a great lover of these bril- liant green stones, and often wears a fire parure of emeralds worth priceless sums, while Queen Maud' of Norway owns a flexible waist-belt made of largo emeralds, over 100 stones being used in the making. Mrs. Willie James has a wonderful trellis-work collar of emeralds, and the Marchioness of Tweeddale possesses a superb col- lection. Princess Duleep Singh has a long neck- Imo of cabochon emeralds set with pearls.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

LAMB CUTLETS EN BELLE VUE. - Take .aa many cutlets as there are well-shaped bones from two necks of lamb, and lard them with tongue or ham; lard level with the cutlet. Braise the cutlets with carrots, turnips, onions, celery, and fine herbs, on a layer of bacon, in a pint- of stock for about an hour and a-half, covering close all the time. Let them absorb the stock till nearly cold, then press each cutlet between two plates; trim neatly, then set them in aspic in a large shallow dish so that each cutlet has a thin mask of jelly on both sides. A cutlet cutter dinned into hot wntor 1,.17qt, then be used to stamp each cutlet out of the aspic. Vish in a circle, fill the centre with a macedoine of vege- table soaked in mayonnaise sauce, and garnish ;with aspic jelly.

WARRINGTON

FOOTBALL.

THE WARRINGTON CLUB.

Secretary's and Treasurer's Annual Report.

The annual statistical report and statement of accounts has this week been issued to the members of the War- ; rington Football Club. Mr. H. Ashton (secretary), in the course of a lengthy ! report, says: The form of the premier teara has been I generally of a most unsatisfactory character. They by , no means upheld their reputation of previous seasonsri and throughout the entire playing portion the I team has—with one or two exceptions—never risen to that high standard of excellency that has characterised their play 'in the past. The primary cause is due to the unprecedented number of accideats to players ; practically in every match players were more or le Ll damaged, sod almost every member of the playing strength of the Club has suffered from injury. The personnel of the team contained six members who resided out of town. This fact was detrimental in one sense, particularly to the Friday night players' meeting—so successful in former years. Consequently the other memberS took little or no interest in these gatherings, thereby creating a careless spirit, whtch eventually permeated the " A " teSra, and on two occasions not a single playing member attended. The players themselves also felt somewhat glorified after their cup win, and relied • upon their reputation too much, inasmuch that during the summer hardly one of them was inclined to practice sprinting, a common feature in previous years. Every inducement was offered but to no purpose, and se3ing that your com- mittee provide running pump-, the fault was not their's The younger element, having no one to set them an example, did not improve in pace, as was hoped, land to this fact must be attributed the reason why many of them appaared to have lost their speed. Pace is a very essential factor, and players must avail themselves of every opportunity to cultivate it if they desire to be- come first-class.

Mr. Ashton also condemns the present League system and advocates areturn to the "two points for a win and one for a draw" style of competition. He adds that 17 fresh players have been signed on during the season and comments favourably on Lees, Taylor, Lambert and Cartwright. He recommends the adoption of the Works Competition touch-rule, the redoction of the numbers of players to. 13 a-side and the "play-the- ball" rule as all being likely to improve the Northern Union game and make it more popular. Mr. T. Pemberton's report on the financial side reads as follows :—ln submitting the accounts for the past seasonl have to report hat the membership has again increased, the number being 852, an increase of 46 over _the previous season. The League as at present con- stituted, together with our having had to play at night, what should be good drawing matches and unsatis- factory and costly fixtures, hare been the means of con- siderably reducing our first team gates—the gross receipts being less by over £l,OOO. The A team, by their continued success have gained in receipts over £36. Taking the total gate money, less payrneats, the net reduction in gate from last year is £605 14s. 2d. The bar profit amounts to £B7 23.1 d. Grr ter interest has been shown in the Works Competition, notwith- sthnding. the cold end inclement weather, and the balance m hand amounted to £49 12s. Ild. from which grants have been tirade to injured players and attendants amounting to £l3 ; Infirmary, 20 guineas; Nurses Institution,lo guineas ; and the St. John Ambulance society, £5. Thirty-five claims have been made upon the Insurance Society, all of which have teen promptly settled. In payments, fares are incre?sed, this being accounted for by the jcurneys to Millom and Barrow and extra journey to Leeds for teams to see the New Zealand match. The item under the beading of wages, dm., has decreased by £303 15s. Id. Taking into con- sideration the adverse circumstances which the com- mittee have met with during the past season, and the lack of enthusiasm displayed in the League matches, I consider that we have come out of the ordeal very satisfactorily. It will be seen from the statement of assets and liabilities that the club still has a credit balance—cash and other securities—amounting to £1,112 19s. 2d. _

The abstract of payments and receipts shows that rents, rates, and taxes absorb £154 16s. 10d. ; players' wages, tranfers, and bonuses, £1235 ss. 6d.; railway fares £142 13s. Stockton Heath Rugby Union Club. The annual meeting of the members of the Stockton Heath Rugby Union Football Club was held at the Stag Hotel, Walton, on Wednesday and was well attended. Formed twelve mon,ls ago the efforts of the club during the first season can fairly be regarded as satis- fr ;tory. Out of twenty matches played eight have been won, eleven lost, and one draa a and a total of 210 points has been scored against 181. This record would doubtless have been much improved had all clubs with whom fixtures had been a,ranged fulfilled their engage- ments. As it was four clubs failed to put in ah appear- ance on the Ellesmere-road ground whilst a similar number were unable to raise teams at home to meet the Heathens. With regard to cancelled matches the club has been very unfortimAte and there has been a strong feeling that the Rugby Union should inflict exemplary penalties upon all clubs which fail to complete their fixty res. UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS.—Advt. Exciting Incident at Latchford. An exciting incident, unfortunately attended with serious consequences occurred at Latchford on Saturday evening. The mail van was on its way from Lymm to Warrin&.on and when at Thelwall the horse became unmanageable and galloped off at top speed. Near the Cheshire Cheese Inn the vehicle collided avith an electric standard and was completely over-turned. The driver, a man named Christopher Murphy, of Lynam, was pinned beneath the van and when extricated it was found that he had a severe wound near the right temple and was bleed- ing freely. He was carried into the Cheshire Cheese Inn, and after receiving attention from Dr. Lead- better was removed in the police horse ambulance to the Infirmary, where he was detained. A little girl named Ellen Worthington, daughter of Henry Worthington, who was passing at the time of the accident, was knocked down and received a cut on the head. She was also attended by Dr. Leadbetter and was afterwards removed home in a serious con- dition suffering from concussion of the brain. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—IINDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Local Motorist's Success. Mr. A. V. Baxter, of Warrington, won the Man- chester Motor Club's third annual climb for motor- cycles up the celebrated Snake Hill, at Glossop, on Saturday. The course was about a mile and a half, starting from the "shooting box corner," and corn- ptitors were allowed 75 yards before reaching the actual starting point, where Mr. H. Fisher officiated. Only 12 of the 25 starters reached the finishing point. The police rendered every assistance, Superintendent Savoury being present, and to whom a vote of thanks was given at the tea. Formula on which results were worked out was :—Time, multiplied by cubical capacity of engine, and divided by total weight of machine and rider, the latter being carefully checked. Results: Ist, Z. V. Baxter, sh.p. uarin-cylinder Vindec bicycle ; 2nd, H. Hurst, tri-car, with single. cylinder White and Ponpe engine ; 3d, A. H. Albert 31. h.p. Bnanrant bicycle ; 4th, W. Newbury, 2 h.p. Minerva bicycle. Baxter took four prizes —Humber vase, club gold medal, ani two special ptizes of 3 guineas and 2 guineas, one of these for fastest net time. The second man took three prizes. Over 100 members and friends were present. Death of Mr. John Sampson.

We record with regret the 'death which took place early on Tuesday morning of Mr. John Sampson, who for many years was landlord of the Norton Arms Hotel, Wilderspool-road. Mr. Sampson had been in fa fling health for some months, but it was only recently that be was confined to his bed. Mr. Simpson, who was rearly 64 yea* of age, came to Warrington from Runcorn at an earli, age. He acted as the outside representative of the old Penketh Brewery Company for some years, and became well known in various parts of Lancashire and Cheshire. Subsequently he acquired the business of the late Mr. George Woolf, wine and spirit merchant, Sankey-street, which he afterwards carried on in Bank-street until about 16 years ago, when he became the landlord of the Norton Arms Rotel. Mr. Sampson had been a member of the Licensed Victuallers' Association for many 'years, and was also a member of the Masonic Society, being con- nected with the Lodge of Lights, 148. For three years, commencing in 1900, he represented the Latchford Ward on the Town Council, retiring at the end of his term. In politics he was a strong Con- servative. Shop. Fires. _

The Examiner, Saturday, May 12, 1906. THE EDUCATION QUESTION.

A PLEA FOR MODERATION.

The Rev. John Yonge and the Religious Controversy.

Preaching at Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening, the ;Rev. John Yonge took for his text the words: "Let I your moderation be known unto all men. The Lord is at hand." (Philippians iv., 5.) He pointed out that the text had been given him by the leading article in the British Weekly " upon the theme of "Christian moderation "—surely a most opportune theme at this highly critical and exciting period in our national life. He had no doubt that in very many Christian churches and pulpits that day, both in prayer and preaching, references would be made to the great debate which would begin in the House of Cmmons on Monday upon the Education Bill. It was no exaggeration to say that the theme of national elementary education now held the principal place in popular attention. And the supremest of all questions which waited national settlement in this matter, was whether any and if so what religious teaching should be given in the elementary schools. Around this subject were gathering the greatest controversy, the fiercest strife, the inteusest feeling. He did not desire on that occasion to discuss the great question itself. The time would probably come when they would have need to let it occupy though and attention evert there. But that night be wanted to follow along the line of Dr. Robert- son Nicoll's article upon " Christian moderation," because he believed that to be the supremest virtue. even as it was the most unrnistak&ble need of the present time. They all knew that the question of religious teaching in the elementaryschools was making multitudes of even- good Christian people terribly excited. They were rushing into print upon it in all the organs of the public press. They were shouting themselves hoarse in public meetings. They were per- mitting.themselves to make use of extravagant, violent , and hostile speech one against another. All the signs were pointing to a

tion of the prAnce of Christ—in submission to whose spirit of authority—and of love, the peace of the Church, and the prosperity of the nation and the world were alone to be found. It needed indeed to become the daily prayer of every Christian patriot in this crisis in the nation's history, that Chris- tian moderation might prevail over all the Churches, and all the parties, and all the people. The highest interests of the nation's present and future generations were bound up in the issue of this controversy. That issue could only be secure and successful if it be the outcome of the peaceful spirit of Jesus Christ.

AFRAID OF THE PEOPLE.

ANGLICAN CHURCH'S PITIABLE SUSPICION, The Rev. J. H. Jowett. presiding on Monday evening lat the assembly of the Congregational Union, said it had become clear that the so-called " National Church " did not represent the nation, and she was now busy, detaching herself from the expressed will aud judgment of the people. It had also become clear that that Church was afraid of the people. The suspicion of the Anglican Church of popularly-elected bodies was pitiable. He expressed his amazement at the violence, nwisdom, and short-sightedness of the Anglican attack upon simple Bible teaching, and when a Vicar could be found saying that there was not enough Gospel in undenorni- nationalism to save the soul of a tom-tit, he felt that reunion was neither possible nor desirable.

CATHOLIC APPEAL TO MR. A. H. CROSFIELD, M.P.

On Sunday evening a largely-attended meeting of Catholics attending the Church of the Sacred Heart was held, under the presidency of Mr. John Gibbins, when it was resolved to send the following letter to Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, n.p.:—" 15, Windsor-strest, War- rington, May 6th,1906. To A. H. Crosfield, Esq., M.P. Dear sir,—We, the members of the Church of the Sacred Heart parish,Liverpool-road, Warrington,hereby call upon you to redeem your promise re the Educa- tion Act, to vote against the second reading of this Bill, as it is most unfavourable to Catholics. The Catholic voters of Warrington had no ides of such a revolutionary Bill being brought forward at the last general election. We also wish to remind you of your letter to our Secretary, dated February 28th. re a peaceable and satisfactory settlement of the education question was impossible unless Catholics had the opportunity given them to teach their own religion in their owa way in school hours, and as the phrase goes, to preserve the Catholic atmosphere in their schools. Our reasons for the rejec - tion of the Bill are (1) a hill of such sweeping magni- tude was not at the last General Election dreamed of by the ordinary electors, and is too complex for their understanding now, so that a mandate for so revolu- tionary a measure cannot reasonably be claimed by the Government ; (2) The Bill steps betwean us and our children, and, as we- conscientiously believe, between our children and Him who said 'Suffer little children to come unto He; ' (3) The Bill is as endowment of Nonconformity exclusively, whether the whole system of conformity, or part only, matters not. (1) The Bill gives to parents, who conscientiously believe that indefinite religion is vain, no guarantee that their chil- dren shall receive definite religious teaching, and we believe that the four-fifths proportion will vanish wher as a business adjustment, the local education authority proceeds, as we believe it will Ao, to allot children school places as near as possible to their homes. (5) The Bill ousts the established tribunals of the country in favour of an irresponsible commission, and of the Board of Education. (6) The Bill is confiscatory and op- pressive and an invasion of our civi I and religious liberties as re-established by the Catholic Emancipation Act.— I am, sir, on behalf of the signed parishioners, yours faithfully, P. J. GEORGE, hon. secretary."

GREAT AND GRIEVOUS FIGHT about the religious teaching in the day schools. There was, therefore, no greater or more urgent need just now than that a spirit of Christian moderation, forbearance, and charity, should be prayed for, sought after, stiiven for, so that the final issue of the controversy might be one of peace rind goodwill. No other issue could possibly be permanent or prosoerous. National educa- tion could never be established on any lasting founda- tion which was not laid in concord and confidence and mutual concession. Was it not fair to assume that this was the conviction or the Government who had made in their education proposals numerous concessions on the question of religious teaching which were intended to make for mutual peace. The hope of settlement, therefore, lay in a universal spirit of concession. Con- cession was the off-spring of moderation—that great Christian virtue for which St. Paul pleaded in the text. There had just comae to light a very stzikine, and very hopeful sigh of the times to which he felt it his duty to call their earnest attention. He confessed that when his eye first fell upon it on Saturday week it theevening paper, it struck him with a feeling of astonishment and even bewildermentit seemed to be such a marvellously incongruous thing to appear now. He had been reading day after day speeches, letters and articles in the Press, from speakers and writers of views diame- trically and even hotly opposed on the education con- troversy. Especially had he been struck and pained,not only by the extravagant uncharitableness but also by the exorabitant and overhearing demands of prelates and priests ; the vengeful threats of ecclesiasticsahe bitter- ness against the Bible, and all the rest of it. He had been oppressed grievously with the fear that this great controversy seemed in danger of making wider and deeper rift than ever between Catholic and Protestant, Churchman and Dissenter, the religious and the irre- ligious. Just in that mood, right in the midst of this ecclesiastical, political storm his eye fell upon a new and even astounding proposal' for fellowship between the Christian Churches in earnest prayer for THE RE-UNION OF CHRISTIANS. In the religious education controversy, surely tney must see the infinite desirableness of CHRISTIAN MODERATION. Who and what were they that would be most consplcu- oats in this great diemission ? It was the individuals and the communities that were most supremely religious. It was the religious question that was going to make the greatest noise. It would be the religious leaders who would be in the forefront of this battle. Then what would the world say, when Christian ministers of whatever church, called by whatever high-sounding , name or title, and when Christian people 'nail com- munities, fell upon one another in wordy warfare and became reckless in method and ii speech? ROCHE'S HERBAL ERIBROCATION.

ROCHE'S FiERBAL EMBROCATION.

IT would appear that the ENGLAND AND occupation of Tabah by the TURKEY, troops of the SULTAN of Turkey is part of a general movement of aggression. The same sort of raid has been made into Persian territory. For some reason or other the SULTAN has persuaded himself or has been persuaded by his Ministers, that this is a good time to enter on a policy of adventure. The recent change of Government in this country. may have something to do with it. ABDUI HAMID may choose to believe the Conserva- tives when they say that they alone are cap- able of a vigorous handling of foreign affairs though his experience ought to have taught him quite another sort of lesson; and, to do justice to the Conservative party on the present I occasion, it is quite firm in its support of Sir Edwean GREY. "The Foreign Minister may count on the full support of the Unionist party," say the Conservative newspapers, and at least one of them has so little faith in the squeezability of Liberal Ministers of which we have heard so much that it advises the SULTAN to climb down at once, " and the sooner the better for himself." There are stories of the bad state of the SULTAN'S health. They may possibly be true, but they are just as probably set about for the purpose of excusing delay in the aforesaid inevitable climbing down process.

For the present the situation is rather threaten- ing though it is difficult to believe that Turkey will not behave on this occasion as she has always previously behaved when she has been resolutely dealt with,but the British Government is evidently preparing for the possibility of persistence in the Turkish claims. Four of our largest battleships are already anchored in Phalerum Bay, together with six cruisers and four destroyers. As a naval demonstration tha. is rather formidable, but I though these precautions against the rejection of the ultimatum suggest an instant readiness to act with great decision and in the most forcible and convincing manner should the occasion arise, Sir EDWARD GnEr is,nevertheless,carrying on negotia- tions with much tact and discretion. He is squeez- ing the sublime Porte without doubt, but afso without unneossary violence. The SULTAN has merely to say that he agrees to an inquiry in- to the frontier question and the difficulty is at once at an end. As to what an inquiry would reveal, there can be no manner of doubt. It is a hundred years or so since Turkey could put forward any reasonable claim to that portion of the Siniatic peninsula which is the occasion of the present

===== 0002601_19060512_art0054.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
_ . _

===== 0002601_19060512_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
dispute. Then, too, the frontier line was deter- mined by a firman in 1892. The Turkish conten- tion now is that the firman had no reference to the territory in this dispute, but this is only a part of that extraordinary effrontery which has been dis- played by the Turkish Government during the negotiations, and which denies to us the right to put what is obviously the just intarpretation on a public document.

Whatever happens, the British Government may safely rely not only on the unanimous sup- port of the country, but on the goodwill of other Governments. It is satisfactory to have received from Germany a definite assurance that she has given no sort of encouragement to Turkey in the raising of this frontier dispute. Her participation to any extent whatever did not seem probable,but there are in this country certain newspapers which insist on perceiving Germany's hand everywhere, and which in the most heedless and determined way endeavour to precipitate a quarrel on the slightest or the most vague excuse. If it were possible to believe that Turkey was not merely "trying it on," but had real and serious intentions, we might be driven to think that she was herself preparing for a war. The value of the territory which she claims is almost purely of a strategical character, but its value in that respect is exceedingly high, seeing how Egypt l and the Suez Canal might be endangered from such a situation. The Government says Sir EDWARD GREY, has exhibited great patience and moderation. It has indeed I And wo shall now speedily know what result these qualities hive pro- duced on the unwise heads that are responsible for Turkish affairs.

There can be no ques- THE FREE CHURCH tioning the fact that Free COUNCIL. Church Councils have

accomplished and are still doing a valuable work in fostering the spirit of unity. Under their influence Free Churchmen of all shades of opinion are learning to sink minor differences, and to stand firm on the great funda- mental principles which all profess. It is encouraging to find that the Warrington Free Church Council is in a very healthy and flourishing state. Its vitality was strik- ingly evidenced by the large attendances at the annual public gatherings held on Thursday and by the great interest and enthusiasm which characterised the proceedings. The movement has made considerable headway in the town and bids fair to become a still greater power for good. One successful undertaking which the Council can point to with pride is the provision of the Free Church Hall at Winwirk which has supplied an undoubted want. Of the three powerful speeches delivered at Thursday evening's meeting, the m.st interesting was that of the President (Mr. C. E. Parker) who is to be warmly congratu- lated on his election to the presidency of the South Lancashire Federation of Free Church Councils. His remarks were entirely devoted to the vexed question of education and he put forth a strong and well reasoned plea for simple Bible teaching in the schools. His speech was 'decidedly moderate in tone and we would commend it to the careful perusal of our readers—Church and Nonconformist alike.

. Most of the 8:.;2 members THE FOOTBALL of the Warrington Football Cum Club will, we think,conclude on perusing their annual reports that the organisation has been particularly well managed during the past season, though they may be disappointed with the bald achievements in a football sense. The past eight months have been a very trying time for Northern Uke°3 Clubs, for the game is either in a transitory state—in which case there is hope—or it is on "its last legs." Certainly, just now there is ample scope for the wail of the possiraist, and unless a marked improvement is shown bath iu the quality of the football and the management of League competitions, the. Northern Union will become a spent force in the sporting world. We are hopeful that such ;a calamity, affecting so many renowned Rugby clubs, will be averted by wise management. So far as the Warring- ton Club is concerned there is no ground for despondency, even though there has been a loss on the year's working of £233. The Club, thanks to the foresight of its rnanagen, is in a remarkably sound position, and the loss just men- tioned is small when the fact is considered that the revenue for the season is over £l,OOO less than a ' year ago. It may seem strange hat it is none the less true, that the great cup victory of 1905 brought no advantage in 1906—rather the reverse. The players have rested on their laurels to a great extent, ind consequently have not shown the prowess in the field that was expected of them. Perhaps the most gratifyiog feature of the season is the manifest improvement of the second string, who have again captured the Lancashire Combination Championship, and put up record scoring figures. Warrington F.C., at any rate, can be trusted to hold its own for some years to come. The only fear is for the parent body,which for some time has not been steering a course to inspire public !confidence and command support.

We have been much amused THE DnINK PROBLEM by reaciing a columu of SOLVED. notes sent us .for oublica7

tion by "The Licensed Trades Literary and Press Agency," which pro- poses to supply us with such delictable literature weekly if we consider it suitable for our columns. Frankly, we don't. The object of the supply is obvious. It is designed to show the heartrending martyrdom to which " the trade " is subjected by legislature and the judiciary, and also to stir up the free-born Briton into saying that he will drink intoxicants when and where he likes. If the aforesaid Briton did not get drunk he might have had this priceless privilege. Not that "the liceased trades" want him to get drunk. Oh, no! They would rather he drank to an unlimited extent without making a beast of himself, but the only way they can suggest to bring about so delightful a consummation for themselves and the Briton is to make facilities for drinking practically unlimited. One of these supplied paragraphs points out that " in Paris, as in any other French town of any size, it is, practically, possible to obtain a drink at any hour of the twenty-four, ands intoxication, except in the very lowest quarters, is very rare. Then we have the moral, emphasised with charm- ing naivette, thitt because drinking facilities are greatly restricted across the border," Scotland is the most drunken country on the face of the earth." Evidently temperance workers are on the wrong tack. They should see that public-houses are built in the proportion of at least one to every row of dwellings and kept open night and day Then the evil would abate and drunkenness bo no more. It is probable that the profits of the trade would increase many-fold, but this interesting fact was probably overlooked in the eager desire to ,preach the new gospel of temperance from the headquarters of the Licensed Trades Literary and Press Agency.

The House of Commons was COMEDY OF Two on Monday, vastly enter LABOUR PARTIEB. tamed by what may fairly be termed "The comedy of the two Labour parties." So far, the I.L.P. and L.R.C. group have been especially prominent, and I have done excellent work in a business-like fashion. , They have even achieved some not inconsiderable triumphs for a new party sitting on the opposition ? side of the House. But apparently Mr. Keir • Hardie and his fo'lowers are forgetting that they are, after all, only a sinall—if very pushful— , 1 minority. So far they have been generously treated : in the matter of representation on Committees of - ' the House, but on Monday they fell foul of the • Chief Government Whip through displaying over- , much acquisitiveness. On the Committee - ,

===== 0002601_19060512_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
on the Housing Acts Amendment Bill there appeared the name of Mr. Henry Vivian, the Liberal-Labour Member for Birkenhead, but no nominee of the Labour party was mentioned. Up jumped Mr. Keir Hardie and said : "it was eminently a committee on which there ought to be a member of the Labour party, yet there was not one." With his first assertion we are in ag,reement,and indeed the Chief Whip only quarrelled with Mr. Hardie in regard to the second. He reminded the Labour leader that there was another section of the Labour party on the Government side of the House with equal claims to representation, and in the person of Mr. Vivian, Labour was represented. The I.L.P.'s and the L.R C.'s did not relish this at all, and wanted to dub the others " Liberals pure and simple. This only resulted in a crushing rejoinder from the Chief Whip. Now it reply, and no doubt does, suit Mr. Keir Hardie and his devoted band to make a distinction between themselves and those professed represen- tatives of Labour on the Government benches. But, in the view of the workers of the country eenerallv, such men as Mr. Fenwick, Mr. Burt,Mr. Enoch Edwards, Mr. Maddison, and Mr. Vivian are as trne disciples of Libour politics as the most hide-bound, contention-pledged meinbers of the I.L.P. or L.R.C. With regard to the case in point a better or truer representative of Labour than Mr. Vivian could not be appointed to such a body as the Housing C im- rnittee. The Member for Birkenhead, who is an old trade unionist, has devoted much time to the study of the housing problem, and is chairman of the Ealing Tenants Ltd., an association of tenant owners who have put their capital together and are developing en estate and erecting. houses of their own. If the Labour group under Mr. Keir Hardie was . anxious to supplant Mr. Vivian with a nominee of its own, then it showed a lamentable avant of devotion to the hest interests of the working-classee. Faction•fighting of this kind will not only retard the Labour movement, but will also render impossible that emancipation of the submerged tenth which should be the aim of every Labourite whether he sits in Opposition or with the Government.

LETTER FROM MR. H. WHITHAM. THE HORRORS OF SAN FRANCISCO Vivid Personal Experiences. WRITER "UNABLE TO ,WORK AGAIN." Mrs. S. Burdett, Old-road, Latohford, yesterdly l'eceived the following letter from her brother, Mr. H. Whitham of San Francisco, and formerly of Warrington:- 11y dear sister and folks generally,—' think you tll have received a telegram before this and will know that our family is quite safe. Our home is els') ranch better than the thousands arAund us. I would like to tell you of the state of affairs here but fear I cannot, as my nervous system seems to be pretty well broken up. Of one thing lam pretty sure, I may never be able to work again, as the use seems to have gone out of my legs, and what the future has in store for me I cannot tell. I must say this, however: there Ire thousands upon thousands very very much worse than we are. I shall try to send papers that will describe the situation much better than I can. This is a city of about 400,009, and its distances are long, and we depend entirely on the street car The cars are all stopped as the are all lifted out of shape an the rails twisted if they had been made of wire. My own personal , were something fearful, and in horror tinded me of the Egypt affair. I was in a large Itt warehouse, and at 5.15 a.m., two or three

things happened. The floor began to lift and tumble about, and the lights all went out, and pieces of freight 'lid things I could hear but could not see. I had to grOPe nIY way to a door, and when I had found one I i con d not open it—all strained out of shape. Then I got to her Worse still. After some time I found one, and with much effort I forced the bolt. Of course I realized that a fearful earthquake bad paid us a visit, and not a friendly one. All this ' time my great anxiety wa„bout my family. What had become of 'them? I was a long way from them and Imust °walk it. When I got into the street I found fire had started and the quake had disrupted the whole of the water system I got home at last and found my family, like all the rest of the People, out in the street. My lot were all dressed, but thousands I had seen in the street with blankets and sheets vcrind them. The scores of houses I had seen lifted right out into the street and the houses that were half over, with people linside ! Remember Itost of the homes are built of wood, and now, talk of The burning of Rome, the fire had got strong hold of city and was working out its sweet will. My house course was not tenable. The whole of the furniture • in heaps on the floor, and we left it to seek one of Itly married daughters who lived a mile away on the , side. We found them all right. They had had a good slake but not much. From this point we had a full view of the citl. The fire was at least a mile away. They had begun to use dynamite, but no use, the fire came steadily along, helped by a gentle breeze from the ocean. People were soon crowding the main streets loaded with bedding. 'They were trying to move furniture to safe places as they thought, but the fire warned them to move on. Immense warehouses, up to ten and twelve storeys high, were in flames. Oh, but it was beginning to look like Hell itself. The old and the young, the aqpless and so on were to care for. Creeping along it swept hospitals, churches, rich mansions—all went md the people came crowding up the hills. Night, on and as we sat on the hill side watching the demon pursue its relentless course, and listened ,work of the dynamiters, we could but think this was tragedy indeed. Well, we must seek a . rest, we were all spent. What a sight when turned out in the morning! The fire had swept tl's rich mansions on Nob Hill out of sight. It had c'tne slowly yet surely through half the city, and I t:11„tlirl see it circling my home, but it did not reach it. Re fire played some strange antics. ,At one time it I°ol3ld seem as if it were going to clear all out in one trectimit ; then it would stop and get hold of another 'at, and so mine escaped. Nopr what we have to tear is disease. All the mains having burst leaves usith ' a water difficulty and makes the imperative getaands of nature a sore trouble. AB if all this were not enough the evil elements of an Francis'eo came to the front and martial law was uoclared. Soldiers, marines and police were given a ll'wer fearful to think of. No lights allowed in AEI houses after dark, no fires to be kindled on the risk of being shots dead. Thugs for robbing the dead, hot on sight. A: tailor told to do something in the .rntreet, refused, shot dead. A woman persisted in light- -I'lR a fire, shot dead. Now, we all light fires in the streets to cook with, and get the food to cook as we i 411. Yesterday I hobbled over a mile to get some 'read and then could only get one very small loaf, taNlot, , gn I said there were six of us. I have to say there is heaps of now, and the people are behaving as one family, ,gring and taking, helping one another. NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Warrington seems to be a training ground in some respects for other towns to benefit by. We are con- tinually losing promising young men, the latest to go being Mr. Ernest Haigh connected with the Education Department. • *** The musical programme given in the Park during the summer months by our local bands is highly appreciated and the opening of the 190 d season will hailed with pleasure. The first performance will be given on Sunday week (May 20th), by the Vounteer band. .

* * * The Accrington Trades and Labour Council are making an effort to secure the appointment of a number of local Labour men to the borough and county magisterial bench. It Thursday's night's meeting of the Council a number of names of suitable candidates were mentioned.

* * * According to Spiritualism, the spirit in sleep always leaves the body ; the body can rest more completely in all its functions without the presence of the spirit. In sleep, too, the spirit actuallY sees, feels, and hears only it cannot always take back to the bodily memory all its experiences.—lsabella a Blackwood in " Mont* Review." * * * It will be news to most people to learn that the National Liberal Club supplies the present Cabinet. Every member of the Liberal Government belongs to the Chill, 283 M.P.'s and 50 members of the Upper House. The Club is in a very flourishing state, and as a result of the General Election has had an acces- sion of 500 members to its large roll.

_ * * Were it not told by.a. Cleveland hunting yeoman of standing, the following story would invite unbelief. On Sunday his shepherd saw a fox cub come from a covert adjoining a field in which sheep and lambs were, and be suckled by one of the ewes whose lambs had both died. It is supposed that the vixen must have died. He told his master, who disbelieved the ' story, but in the evening he saw the same sight. *s* Judging by the great interest with which Mrs. Leo Grindon's lecture was listened to on Wednesday eivening, Warrington should soon boast numerous " green oases in dingy places." Mrs. Grindon,who is a thorough enthuiast, gave some extremely practical hints which we have no doubt will be readily acted upon by those who are anxious to improve the appear- ance of the town.

._ ** *

The school children are taking up the Beautiful Warrington movement with splendid enthuisiasm and there are no fewer than 2,500 members of the juvenile section. There was quite an encouraging muster of boys and girls at the lecture on Wednesday evening. We wonder whether they will act on Mrs. Leo Grindon's advice and petition the Education Com- mittee to supply creepers for the ornamentation of the school premises. * * Miss Ella Champion, of the Camberwell School of Art, has designed an artistic badge for the Labour party, the central idea of which is a picture illustra- tion of the worlds, "Labour, either of pick, pen, or shovel, is the heart of the world." Will the badge be available for both sections of the Labour party in the House of Commons or will Mi. Keir Hardie and his friends claim it as their own ? . . .

Amateur gardeners who have practised their hobby on the waste ground adjoining the lines between Warrington and Padgate, have certainly imnroved thl appearance of the ground,and will,no doubt,later when the " crops " come up receive the fruits of their labour. Gardening of this nature is a sensible "hobby" for it is not only good for the health, but there is also a reasonable prospect of a fair percentage on the out- lay. The succulent potato appears to be the favourite vegetable, but a few have brightened up their little plots by flowers. * * *

The following exquisite passage on education appeared in the leading article of the "Birmingham Daily Mail" on May Bth :—" The elementary educa i - tion n this country, as we have previously poihted out, is of dual character—Church schools and Board schools. No child is compelled to attend the former kind of schools, and that is why they are known as voluntary schools." We wonder if the office-b wrote the leading article on that day. Such elemen- tary knowledge of elementary education suggests some such solution.

* * * In another colura we are enabled to publiska letter from Mr. Henry Whitham,which tells in vivid language the story of horror from San Francisco. Mr. Whitham and his family are all safe, but we are very sorry indeed to learn that our esteemed correspondent is suffering from a nervous breakdown and has almost lost the use of his legs. The fear that he will be unable to work again must be almost the severest catastrophe of the lot to Mr. Whitham. We hope that the return of normal conditions in the re-constructed city may bring about an improvement in his health. The late Dean of Manchester was well-known in Warrington, and very warmly respected for his rugged honesty and breezy good humour. A fine tribute was paid to his memory by the Bishop of Manchester on Tuesday when he said that their "beloved Dean' was a Lancashire man, baunderstood Lancashire men, and loved the Church of England to the core. "He was one," added his lordship, "whose counsels, whose wisdom, and whose honest and sincere desire to do what was right and just and equitable was a stand-by' to us all, in times of doubt and difficulty." * * * The Railway-station Beautiful is riot quite such 3 long way off as it may seem. The Cheshire Lines Committee have long been known for the interest dis- played in all that relates to the floral decorations of their stations. At the instance of the general manager so we learn from this week's issue of "The Gardener," a large number of ornamental trees and shrubs have been placed on the platforms of and at the entrance to Lord-street, Southport. The arrangement is quite effective, and with the aid of a few hanging baskets this station during the season will be quite in keeping with the beautiful street in which it is situated. When, we may ask, will it be the turn of Warrington Central Station? . .

* * Mrs. Leo Grindon told an amusing story when lecturing under the auspices of the Beautiful War- rington Society on Wednesklav evening. It seems that at certain schools in Manchester the authorities have provided creepers for the beautifying of the walls, and the plants are placed in charge of the scholars. Mrs. Grindon, of course, takes a great interest in this work, and on visiting one of the schools to see how the creepers were progressing she found that some miscreant had dared to pull them up. The children however, hastened to assure her that no scholars from • that school were responsible for the danlage, but that it had been done by boys attending the Sunday school ! So much for religious instruc- tion.

* * * Lord Hugh Cecil is an earnest educationist, but he is an even more earnest High Churchman. We hope the people of Warrington will flock to heo.r him this (Friday) evening. If they are unbiased on the religious question they will probably come away con- vinced that what Lord Hugh Cecil and his followers tnost desire for the elementary scholar is not an efficient grounding in the three R's but the inculcation of their own particular religious dogmas. The sort of school that Lord Hugh really wants was once capitally hit off by " F.O.G. " in, a famous cari- cature which depicted the day school and the Church standing back to back with a door leading from one to the other. Those who go to the meeting to night will probably hear very little about real education, but a good deal about "the sacrednes9 of trust-deeds" and the attack on the Church of England. * * * Sixty towns and cities, including Warrington, have already issued placards warning their inhabitants aaainst the dangers of indulgence of intoxicating drinks. Yet there are certain interested parties who still think it worth while to raise protests against the spending of ratepayers' mons%) for such a purpose, and quote " eminent K.C.'s-" in support of their futile arguments. Da these agitators on behalf of private, as against public interest, 'really believe that the town clerks anti town councils of such important cities as Leeds and Liverpool—to say nothing of Birmingham—would have sanctioned such an illegal expenditure of public money, however ardently the medical officer had recommended his scheme, on behalf of the public health? The objection is frivolous, but it shows how amxious " the trade "is to discredit any scheme which may result in people either taking no intoxicants at all, or imbibing such a limited quantity as cannot adversely affect their health. We hope Town Councils generally—more enlightened than that of Macclesfield—will pursue the policy of educating the people in the dangers of alcoholic excess. There can be no question that public health considerations demand this and many other precautionary measures.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

NEW WORKS FOR WIDNES.

-- • • • Widnesians will be glad to hear that a new works is about to be started in Widnes. The name of the firm is Greensvay Brothers, of Bilston, near Wolverhampton. They are manufacturers of galvanised iron, and will employ between one and two hundred men. They have taken over the VicOaria Chemical Works, lately occu- pied by Bowman and Co. SUDDEN DEATHS AT WIDNES.

John Ryan, an elderly man, who resided at 44, Bridge. street, Widnes, was found dead in bed at seven o'clock on Monday morning. He bad been suffering from bronchitis for some time and had been attended by Dr. Hutchinson.—John Jones, a young ex-soldier, who resided with his married sister at 3, Travis-street, Widnes,lslied suddenly on Monday morning about nine o'clock. He had complained of feeling ill since Satur- day but nothing serious was anticipated. He had not deen medically attended SMART FINES FOR WIDNES YOUTHS. At Widnes, on Monday morning, Sarnael Bott, Beau mout-street, I Thomas M'Neese, Viaduct-street, and Alfred Stokes, Back James-street, were charged with stealing a bag, value 4d., the property of Thomas Gerrard, sod also with stealing a quantity of scrap iron value Is. 6d. the property of the Hutchinson trustees, from the Widnes Dock. M'Neese stole the bag from Gerrard's ofrice,and all went down to the dock and took the iron. All pleaded guilty, and were lined 10s. and costs for stealing the bag, and 21 each for participating in the iron theft.

LICENSING BUSINESS AT RUNCORN.

The following licences were transferred at the Run- corn Sessions on Monday: Royal Hotel from George Curzon to Matthew Macdonald; Boat House Inn from Alfred James to Charles Baker ; Camden Hotel from Alfred Curzon to Richard Marshall ; Foresters Arms from Margaret Ann Forshaw to Harry Warburton ; and Rutters Vaults from Henry Shepherd to James Brad- ley. Temporary authority was granted to Patrick Regan to sell at the Brunswick Vaults in lien of Charles Baker Pugh.—Mr. F. A. Lake, on behalf of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Company applied for sanction to structural alterations to the Anchor Inn, George Horse- field, licensee.—Mr. Fred Broady, the company's agent. said it was proposed to convert three detached stables into one seven stall stable. There would be no increase of the drinking facilities.—lnspector Egerton raised no objection and the application was granted.—Mr. W. H. :Tones, agent for Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, applied for sanction to structural alterations at the Man at the Wheel. He said it was proposed to use a store room as a club room.—Mr. F. J. Jones, architect, Runcorn, sub- mitted the plan, and on the understanding that the room would be used only by members of the club the applica- tion was granted. WIDNES COUNCIL. r SCAVENGERS: THEIR WORK AND PAY. The Mayor (Alderman S. Qiunn) presided over the. monthly meeting of Widnes Council held on Tuesday. —On the Health Committee minute., Mr. Boyle drew attention to the nightsoil men. He stated that they worked an eight hours' night at the rate of 4s. a night. Judging fronuthe character of their work, he did not see that they received remunerative wages, and he should suggest an increase of Is. per shift. He also, argued that it cost a man on night work more to live than one on day work. In answer to this, Mr. Hemingway stated that the men never complained about their work, and the Committee judged from this that they were well satisfied.— Alderman S. Owens. in moving the adoption of the Finance Committee's minutes, said that the committees estimated an expenditure for the year of £17,336, as against £17,390 last year. Economical working had saved on last year's working £205; so that instead of the general district rate being 2s. 10d. in the pound, they would only levy a rate of 2s. 9d. The Highway Committee was the only one that bad overspent, and this, he said, augured well for the committees' economy. One of the items showed a marked decrease in hospital maintenance, where the coat had been materially reduced, whilst the number of patients slightly exceeded those attended to in previous y•ars (hear, hear).—Mr. Welding asked if the Corporation I would obtain a dividend from the Transporter Bridge Company this year, to which Alderman Owens replied ' that he hoped that in his estimate for next year some funds from that source would be included, but he could not promise them any this year.—The Mayor jocularly reminded Mr. Welding to ask the same question next year to the day.

ST, HELENS.

ST. HELENS PUBLIC BATHS.

The St. Helens Water Committee on Wednesday ap- proved the estimate for the coming year,under which it is proposed to charge the rates with £SOO for the Boundary- road Baths and £:334 for the Warrington-road Baths, the figures in each case being less than last year. NEW RIFLE RANGE AT ST. HELENS.

The St. Helens Volunteers are erecting a new minia- ture rifle range in their drill field at headquarters. The new range is of the latesG safety pattern, with side and top shields, which render it perfectly sife. and it is laid out for distances corresponding to no, 500, and 600 yards.

LARGE SEWAGE SCHEME.

The St. Helens Health Committee on Wednesday con- sidered the plans for the new scheme of main sewers for Derbyshire Hill, Parr, Fleet-lane, Sutton, and Marshall's Cross, which have been prepared by Mr. G. J. C. Broom, the borough surveyor. The scheme is estimated to cost £48,000. After a lengthy discussion the plans were passed.

TARTS AND BOOTS, STOLEN. At St. Helens, on Wednesday, s, youth named Ernest P. Gamer, of 16, Twyford-street, admitted a theft from the house of Mrs. Allen, a next-door neighbour. Mrs. Allen said that on Monday morning last,about five o'clock, she was awakened by the noise of the back door closing. She went downstairs, but found all right, except that they had forgotten to lock the door. Later she missed some tarts and a pair of boots, and gave infortuation at Dromgoole's pawnshop, where the lad afterwards tried to pledge the boots. Garner said he stole the things because he had nothing to eat. The magistrates said they would give the lad a chance, and he was bound over to come up for judgment. ST. HELEN'S YOUTH'S SLEEP-WALKING

--•— Thomas WCormick, a young man, of 64, Bold-street. made an enterprisine sort of defence on Tuesday when charged before the St. Helen's magistrates with being drunk and disorderly the previous evening. He said the constable only found him in their backyard. He went up to bed, but must have got up in his sleep and have fallen through the window, and the policeman happened to be there. The duief Constable said M'Cormick had been convicted seventeen times, and was a very bad lot. The beach inflicted a fine of 14s. or fourteen days for the drunkenness, and ordered M'Cormick to and sureties for three months or go to gaol for twenty-eight days.

HUSBAND AND WIFE SENTENCED.

A case of theft, in which there were a number of painful circumstances, was tried at St. Helens on Satur- day, when John and Mary A. Leyland were the prisoners. The evidence was that the man and woman took apartments at 59, Brook-street, and stayed there for a fortnight. They disappeared on the morning of Saturday, the lith ult., when it was found that a large number of articles had been stolen and pawned. The woman was arrested in Castleford(Yorkshire) on Wednesda.y,and the man at a glassworks at Conisborough the following day. Mrs.Leyland.the mother,gave evidence to the effect that the husband was very lazy and wonld not work,and thathis wife had been practically starving during the twelve months she had been married to him.—Leyland said he worked at a local glass bottle works up to three months ago.—The Chief Constable said both defendants had been convicted, and the man's employers said be was a most worthless character, and too lazy for them to think of giving him a chance of working for thorn again.—They were each sentenced to two months. ST. HELENS MAY DAY SHOW.

The annual show and competition for horses and i turnouts took place at St. Helens on Saturday in some- what unfavourable weatber. The tradesmen of the town turned out in good numbers, and there was a great num- ber of Corporation horses and vehicles. The Mossbank Prize Band played seleotions in front of the Town Hall. The procession afterwards paraded the town.—Colonel W. W. Pilkington gave a silver cup for the cleanest and neatest turnout for heavy horses. Mr. Joseph Apple- ton gave a cup for light horses, and the St. Helens Farriers' Association also gave a cup. Mr. J. Willis was again the chairman of the committee, with Mr. W. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

EARLESTOWN GROCERS' ASSOCIATION.

The general meeting of the above Association was held on Tuesday last week in the smoke room at the Town Hall. There was a very good attendance. Mr. R. Molyneux (President) in the chair, welcomed Mr. Ireland (Howe and Co.) as a new member. It was resolved that the L. and N.-W. Railway Company be asked to issue market tickets from Golborn, Lowton, St. Helens Junction and Collins Green at reduced fares as is now done with other towns; also to ask the South Lancashire Tramway Co. to run their motor bus to Earlestown, instead of only to Newton, which, it was considered, would benefit the town generally.

On Wednesday night a numerously attended public meeting was held in Wagrave Schools,to. discuss the education proposals of His Majesty's Government. DEATH OF MR. F. GLOVER.

The death took place on Saturday April 28th, at 22, Earle-street, Earlestown, of Mr. F. Glover, at the age of 65 years. The deceased had been for 38 )years in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Company (Wagon Department), and for 35 years was an inspector of rolling stock in the north-western district. He was much esteemed by the chief officers of the com- pany and his fellow workers. Mr. Glover was a quiet, methodical man, whose genial friendship was a pleasure to all who knew him. He was a member of the Loyal Viaduct Lodge of Oddfellows, and was associated with the social rather than the political side of the Conservative Club. The funeral took place in wet weather on Wednesday afternoon, the coffin and hearse being covered with beautiful floral tributes. The Odd fellows were represented by Messrs. J. IV. .Thorp, P.P.G.M.'s T. Tuustall, W. Sutton, and P. Connor D.G.M. W. Devereux, P.O. Lomas, NO. J. Monks. The Conservative Club delegates included Messrs. IV. Sutton, W. Pilling, J. Smith, W. A. Mawdsley, T. Bowes and G. Bailey. Rev. A. Ainley officiated. The funeral arrangements were in the hands of Mr. H. Walton, Cross-lane. MEDICAL OFFICER'S QUARTERLY REPORT. Dr. H. E. Watkins, medical officer of health for Newton-in-Makerfisld, in the course of his report on the health of the township for the first quarter of the year stated that there were registered during the quarter 122 births-77 males and 45 females—and 65 deaths, giving a natural increase of 57 for the quarter. 1 baring the quarter 35 cases of infectious diseases were notified, these being equal to an infectious sick-rate of , 7.3 ; for last quarter the rate was 1736, and for the first quarter of 1905. 10.28. The health of the district had again improved. Nearly all the rates were lower this quarter than last. Most gratifying were the lowering in the infantile mortality, zymotic death and sick rates. The birth-rate was considerably lower ; in the first and second quarters of the year it was usually highest. The death-rates concerning phthisis and other respiratory diseases were higher, but not unusually so for the first quarter. The changes in the rates from last quarter were as follows :—Birth-rate decreased from 31.51 to 25.47 ; death-rate decreased from 1457 to j 3.57; infantile death-rate decreased from 136.05 to 106-5 ; zymotio death-rate decreased from 2'14 to .41 ; phthisis death-rate increased from .61 to 1.87 • respiratory death-rate increased from 2.14 to 375. 'ln regard to scarlet fever he was happy to be able to report a great improvement during the quarter. Measles, which almost disappeared from the township in the early part of the last quarter, appeared in the Wargrave Ward late in this quarter and had assumed epidemic proportions NIEETLNG OF THE NEWTON URBAN COUNCIL - - •

The monthly meeting of the Newton Urban Council was held on gonday evening. Mr. T. Ormand presided and there were also present : Dr. Patterson, Messrs. A. Smith, L. E. Pilkington, M. Seton, J. N. Siddeley, F. Harwood, E. T. Evans, C. B. F. Borron, G. F. Clarke, C. Cole (clerk), R. T. Suttees (water engineer), A. Bowes (surveyor). The Chairman announced with feelings of deep regret the death of Mr. W. Gorse, an official of the Council in the collector's office, which occurred late on Sunday night. The late Mr. Gorse had been a Council official for over nine years, and they now recognized the loss of a faithful and trustworthy servant. But if they felt their loss so keenly what would it mean to the widow and Sour children! The members would all agree that the bereaved widow should be accorded their cordial sympathy and covadolence, and he moved a resolution of condolence. The motion was passed in silence.—On the proposition of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Smith, Mr. N. Siddeley was unanimously appointed overseer in the place endered vacant by the death of Mr. Simpson.— Sub-committees were appointed as follows: -Gas Sub- committee: Messrs. The sheath-knives that most sailors carry with them are, it is said, so highly magnetised as to be capable, if brought within 181 n. of the compass, or deflecting the needle quite two points either way. It is suggested that no man should be allowed to wear one of these knives while steering a vessel or on the look-out in the bridgehouse.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON. ---- 1 44 How -to make Green Oases in Dingy Places."

Gratifying evidence of the progress of the Beautiful Warrington movement was afforded by the large attend- ance at the Co-operative Hall on Wednesday evening when Mrs. Leo Grindon, of Manchester, delivered a highly interesting lecture on " How to make green oases in dingy places." The hall was quite filled, the audience including many members of the juvenile section and a good muster of day school teachers. Canon MORLEY STEVENSON presided and said they were looking forward to holding their annual meeting on June 27th, and he thought he might say withont any too confident boasting, that they would be able to pre- sent a very creditable account of the work of the Society in its fitst year. (Applause.) They bed 2,500 members in the day schools of Warrington, and in that connection he wanted to make a little appeal to the generosity of some of their friends. Those 2,500 children each wanted a plant. They were prepared to provide the pots, but the Society was pledged to find the plants and would find them. At the same time he thought there must be many of their friends with large gardens who would be able to spare a few plants for that pur- pose. In that event. the Society would be extremely grateful if they would communicate with the Secretary (Mr. J. Fairhurst). They hoped in the month of July to have theii• first flower show, not so much with the object of providing a display of magnificent plants, but to show what people could do in their own homes and gardens. With regard to the subject of the lecture, they who lived in smoky towns—which still were smoky though they hoped they would not be for long— wanted to knew what sort of plants they could best grow, how they could best grow them, and to what best advantage they could lay out their garden plots and arrange their window boxes. He did not think they could find anybody in this part of England who conld help them to answer those questions better than Mrs. Leo Grindon. Her name was a household word in Man- chester for the promotion of all that had to do with the growth and beauty of plants and the cultivation of plants in the most unlikely surroundings. If they had had the opportunity of seeing, as he had a short time ago, Mrs. Grindon's back garden in the heart of Man- chester and inspectirv, the really wonderful things that were growing in it, they would understand that she her- self had solved the problem and was therefore the best person to help them to solve it. (Applause.) Mrs. LEO GRINDON prefaced her lecture by describing how she had tended hsr own garden in Manchester. When the late Mr. Leo Grindon—of whose great hive of plants and flowers they would have heard—became too feeble:to visitithe parks he set to work to make a garden in the back yard and in the course of one season it was made so pretty that they could not only sit there in comfort but have their meals in it. That garden was practically in the heart of Manchester, and what could be done in one season there could certainly be done in other people's back yards. It seemed to her a remark- ably happy thing that so many people should have gathered tegetber in Warrington to find out how they might cultivate beauty, for after all there was no greater thing in the whole world than beauty. In work of this kind it was impossible for one person to accomplish much. It was by the united I efforts of a large number that the goal could be attained. When starting upon a fresh undertaking it seemed to her well to bear in mind the story of the talents as told in the Bible for there was nothing more encouraging. They in Manchester, Warrington and I other places had only the one talent but because they could only cultivate a few pretty things and not as Imany as the people who lived in better surround- ' logs, they had despised it. The one talent had been so far forgotten that the cry now was that 1 they had no talent at all and no chance of growing anything. The majority said there was no possibility of green lanes in towns. They must wait a bit and not be so sure of what they could not do. Nature, that ' dear old mother, never wholly forsook the homes of those who loved her. They might get as beautiful green growth in the heart of a city as could be found anywhere. Mrs. Grindon instanced hew in a small back yard off Deansgate, Manchester, a splendid creeper might be seen growing from a window box. Mrs. Grindon advised her hearers to commence their efforts at beautifying by growing the Virginian creeper. Thererwas no drawback whatever to the cultivation of that plant which was grown in great profusion in London. That was the creeper she wanted everyone present to start growing the following day. The creeper was the easiest and quickest in its growth, and could be obtained for 4d., sd. or 6d. As a second plant she advised common ivy which would add much to the beauty of a house, and as a third the dicentra or close clinging creeper. It was also well to try a root of hops as in some places that could he more advantageously grown than the others. Mrs.Grindon gave some useful hints as to the care of the plants, pointing out the great importance of watering them freely. She also showed how window boxes could be used to the best effect. She gave the names of a large number of the hest flowers for cultivation, including the monkshood, yellow evening primrose, London pride, hollyhock, sun- flower, French marigold, primroses, rock plants, &c. The elder, she said. could also be trained to grow against the wall with beautiful effect. Mrs. Grindon concluded a very practical lecture by expressing the hope that Ina year or two her hearers would invite her to drive round to their back gardens and see the pro- gress they had made. (Applause.) Miss BESSIE DUTTON moved a vote of thanks to Mrs. Grindon for her eminently useful lecture. She said that Mrs. Grindon would no doubt from the bent of her intellect have many temptations to go rather !above them, but she had net done so in the least. She had come down to their level and had given them practical knowledge which they would be glad to put into use. She hoped all the children present would try and culti- vate some flowers. Miss Dutton instanced how when she lived in Liverpool she was connected with a Widow Gardening Society which did s splendid work, distribut- ing plants which the people cultivated and then holding an exhibition in St. George's Hall. Mrs. GARNETT seconded and expressed her pleasure at seeing so many young people present. The Penketh and Sankey Window and Cottage Garden Society had been trying io interest children in the work, and she hoped that in Warrington the children- would be able to take a great part in the forthcoming flower show. As regarded the growth of creepers, that was one of the things she had been struggling for in the Penketh neighbourhood for some time arid this year she had added a little prize for the best creepers. She had also been putting in a very strong plea for the planting of trees. They had not, however, as many trees'at Fenketh as they had at Stockton Heath. (Laugh- ter.) She thought landlords and farmers were rather to blame for the scarcity because in widening roads and straightening fences they often cut down trees. Whether they would get trees in the streets of Warrington she did not know. Perhaps they would when the streets were 'widened. She hoped there would be no opposi- tion to it. A tradesmen told her he had been opposed in the matter of putting in the streets tubs containing shrubs, but she thought the town might be much improved in that way. They had the shrubs in front of Widnes Town Hall. and if they could be grown there was ought to have them too. (Applause). The vote of thanks was accorded with great heartiness Mrs. DRTNDON, in responding, said she wanted the school children to join in sending a request to the Education Committee that they might have their school walls planted with creepers. That had been done at some of the Manchester schools, and each plant was placed in charge of a certain scholar. ,
