NOTES, ON GAMES AND ATHLETIC& Cricket.

So far this season cricket locally has been very flat, not through the fault of players, but because the climatic conditions have been such that all the earlier matches have had to be abandoned. The works com- petition opens this (Friday) evening, and will witness ' a new and interestiQg departure in local cricket circles. There is no doubt as to the games being productive of excitement if not of scientific cricket, as each team is to bat for an equal length of time, the team with the highest total to be the winner. This promises to effec- tually put a stop to tedious stone walling, and as hard hitting will be the order, the games should be brief and breezy. The teams which meet to-night are Greenall Whitley and ‘Thitecross.

===== 0002601_19060512_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

Bowling opens in earnest to-day (Saturday) and in connection with the %Works Competition a highly successful season is anticipated. The interest in the contest has increased and Via number of entries has advanced from 27 to 34 teams; while about 800 players have registered their names. To-day's matches in the first round should produce close finishes. P. and K.. (N 0.3 team) should qualify for the succeeding stages of the contest at the expense of Thewlis and Griffiths and it is probable that Monks' Hall (No. 2) will be knocked out of the running by 'Wands' (No. 2) The severest struggle will be between Hutchings and Fletchers (N 0.2 and it is difficult to forecast the victor though Fletchers' are rather the fancied team.

-_ __

The proposal of the Warrington and District League to this season, arrange an extended competition on the knock out principle has met with approval but not with support. The outside clubs which have been approached' have all agreed as to the excellence of the idea but unfortunately few have been able to see their way to join in the contest. It is now a question as to whethe with the limited number of clubs ideutifted (7) with the league, it would nut be better to continue on the old league lines. Certainly the contest would be more interesting than on the knock out rule but there is a feeling that the new idea is the best as by its adoption the greens will not be so frequently closed to bowlers who are not on the teams.

Cycling.

Warrington cyclists, who have beau devotees of the pastime ten years or more will be pleased to hear again of " Jack "Waddington. At one time he was the best- known distance rider in the district and had a shop in St. James-street. He was a regular attendant of the Warrington C.C.'s club runs, and aas one of that type of rider whom nothing could dismay. On Saturday "Jack "turned out in the Manchester Wheelers Fifty miles time trial,and the conditions being at their worst he naturally came in first, and did the fastest time.

ST. BARNABAS' CHURCH.

Sale of Work in Aid of the Extension

Fund.

The Assembly Room at the Conservative club pre- sented a pleasing scene on Wednesday afternoon when a two days sale of work in aid of the St. Barnabas' Church extension fund was opened by Mrs. John White. Thanks to the efforts of a willing band of workers, the room had been very tastefully decorated and the stalls, which were laden with a groat variety of useful and fancy wares, were attractively arranged. The object of the sale was one which specially commended itself to the support of the parishioners. Although of late years many improvements hive been effected in the interior arrangements of the church, the building still remains lin an unfinished state. It was felt that the time had arrived for the raising of the nucleus of an extension fund and the proceeds of the sale will be devoted to that purpose. New features of the sale were two splendid stalls provided by the members of the Bible classes who had been very active in collecting a line display of articles. The following is a list of the stall-holders :—Refreshment stall,lfesdames F. Jones, W.Jones,Grounds,T.Proud,Greenhalgh,Woodcock,Misses 1 Gerrard,E. and S. Whitehouse, and Grounds ; toy stall, Misses Fry and Miss Cole ; No.l work stall, Mrsiddon, 11 Mrs. John McDowell, Miss Short : No. 2 work stall, I Mesdames Connell, Birchall J. Glassbrook and Miss Bolton ; No. 3 work stall. Turnor. as secretary ; young women's 'Bible class. Miss Blakelonk and members of the class ; flower stall, Mrs. A. E. Hands, and Miss Alice White- house. A handsome oil painting of" Glen ',quick," by Mr. J. Parkinson, Lovely-lane, was on view and was roneh admired.

There was a good attendance at the opening of the sale by Mrs. John White, the Vicar (Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth) presiding. Amongst those present were —the Revs. E. C. E. Carleton, H. R. Wild and T. W. Barber end Mr. John White.

The VICAR in explaining the object of the sale said the church was very incomplete, as it lacked a chancel, organ, side aisles, and peal of bells. The funds this year allowed of the proceeds of the sale being devoted to the lextension fund. It was impos- sible for him at present to say what form the fund would take, but if nothing prevented him he hoped soon to be able to make on announcement. He thanked all who had worked for the success of the project, and specially welcomed the two new Bible class stalls. He expressed his indebtedness to all those xvho had attended the working. parties or joined the savings club—withont which he was afraid they would he nnable to hold the sale. In calling upon Mrs. White to open the sale, the Vicar paid a tribute to her willingness to assist worthy objects. Mrs. Mem said they would all be fully agreed that the time had arrived when an earnest effort should be made to complete the church, the foundation stone of which was laid nearly twenty years ago by Lord Winmarleigh. She hoped the sale would not only result in the raising of a large sum of money but would help to remind people that St. 13arnahas Church was stilt incomplete. She trusted the whole of' the funds reonired would soon he forthcoming. (Applause.) Mr. J. TDDON7, (vicar's wardanl moved a vote of thanks to Mrs. White and said it would be some recompense to her for her attendance to know that she was assistinc such a laudable object. They had somewhat improved the church, but the time had arrived when it should be extended and they all trusted that this effort would boa great success. The vote was heartily accorded and the business of the sale commenced.

Encb clay seleCtiona were given by Miss Turner's mandoline band and Miss Plinston contributed several sonu,s.

ALLEGED EXTENSIVE DRAPERY ROBBERY.

At the Borough Police Chun on Monday, William Campbell, Lythgoes-lane, and Charles and Edward Woods, Howard-street, were charged with breaking and entering the shop of Edwin Ambrose Sone& 85,Priestlev- streat, boeween May 3rd and 4th, gnd stealing 75 yards of flannelette and other articles of the value of 214 10s. —lt was stated by Superintendent Monaghan that the shop was safely locked up on the night of May 3rd, but the following morning a large quantity of drapery was missed, an entrance having been effected by removing the catch of the back window. The stolen property was removed from Howard-street to the house of a Mn Dickinson, in Lythgoes-lane, where the police found it. The men made various statements when arrested Mary Dickinson was charged with receiving the property knowing it to have been stolen, but Campbell said the women was innocent.—All the prisoners were retnanded until Saturday, and Mrs. Dickinson was allowed bail.

FREE CHURCH YEAR BOOK.

London : National Free Church Council (8 x5l, 400 pp. 2s. Ed. net). Among the reference books which are so necessary to Nonconformists a high place is taken by the Free Church Year Book. The DOG issue, in addition to the full official report of the Birmingham Council meetings and a review of the year's work, contains much valuable information such as can be obtained from no other one source, including articles on the origin and distinctive features of Denominations ; Legal Information for Free Churchmen, the Free Church on the Mission Field, &c. The statistical tables again thew much increase in the ranks of the Free Churches, and the figures showing the comparison between the Free Churches and the Anglican church are instructive, whilst a list of the names and addresses of the 200 Free Church members of Parliament in a welcome item. The com- pilers have arranged a mass of information invaluable to all Free Churchmen. The book is bound in a tasteful cloth gilt, and a copious index enhances its usefulness.

The Ninetieth Anniversary of the Peace Society will take place on Tuesday, May 22nd. The Public Meeting will be held in the evening at the City Temple, Holborn Viaduct, , B.C. The President Dr. Robert Spenoe Watson, will occupy the chair, and will be supported by Miss Frances Thompson (Birkenhead), J. McCallum, Esq., M.P., P. A. Molten°, Esa., M.P., Rev. Canon Fleming, 8.D., Rev. G. Friedlander (Western Synagogue. Haymarket, S.W.), Rev. T. Rhondda Williams (Bradford, Yorks). Rev. Thomas Yates (Ken- sington), and others. Holders of tickets, which may be obtained from the Secretary, 47, New Broad Street, 8.C.. will be admitted from 6 p.m., and there will be an organ recital from 6.30 until 7, when the meeting com- mences.

An ironmongery firm in a town in the Western States advertised a prize of £2 to the person who, on a certain day, brought into the place and deposited in front of the firm's store the largest number of people, on one vehicle. But at the last moment there appeared a singular vehicle, 83ft. long and oft. wide. It moved on eight wheels, and was drawn by eight horses.. From it descended 110 people.

The German State railway ia much tempted to encourage the Emperor to travel aa often as possible, for each journey he takes is ,a con- siderable sum in the packet of the natinn. His Majesty travels in great splendeur. As a rule, there are two special trains, one for the Emperor and one for the Empress. These are the property of the Prussian State. but ,the travelling expenses are paid by the Emperor himself. The Court trailla are charged at the same rate as ordinary special trains. Thus, the journey from Berlin to Elbing, near the north- east frontier, costs ratller over 5:2.00, and the some fee is, of course, charged for the return • journey. •

In a window of ra. stare in St. Louis, Missouri, are four ,bottles which are believed to be the largest ever blown from glass. The largest is 6ft. 41n. high, and will actually hold the con- tents of 1,500 small perfumery bottles. The smallest, however, is over aft. in height, and will hold' 250 pints. To make the largest bottle no lees than 501 b. of liquid glass was required, which had to be blown into an immense bubble which afterwards coaled into the receptacle. The blowing was done by three men, one reliev- ing the other as soon as he had exhausted his strength.

OUR CLEVER DOCTOR

tALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

BY LITCY HARDY,

Author of "A Madman's Mistake," "The Shadow on the Moor," "The West Room," "The Watcher in the Church Porch," &c.

It was a bleak winter's evening on Dartmoor, and as Horace Thor°ld stood by his fire drawing on his gloves and listening for the sound of his gig coming round to the door, the young doctor was inclined to wonder—as many another country Esculapius has done before him—why it is that patients in outlying districts always seem to select the worst nights for entering and leaving the world and requisitioning the services of a medical man. Horace Thor°ld had sihnly taken up his residence in Devon some six months previously, when an old uncle, of whom he had known very little, had died, and bequeathed all his small property, includinz his medical practice, to the nephew who, it .seemed, had unwittingly pleased him by following the old man's own profession. Horace, who was struggling in London in the s ,rnewhat hopeless attempt to build up a practice for himself (having no capital wherewith to p rchase one), had decided that his wisest course w add be to settle down, at least for a time, in his itticle's place; and had accordingly taken possession of the quaint yet cosy old house upon the moor, with its antiquated furniture, and stepped into his uncle's shoes, as he phrased it, as regards the practice. Old Dr. Thorold's was certainly an "extensive connection" in one sense, as it embraced all the scattered farmsteads and houses within riding distance. The old man had been popular with his patients, and his nephew, who was both clever and painstaking, more than inherited ;his kinsman's good repute. The wintry blast rattled outside, and Horace drew back the curtain and opened the window for a moment to see if snow had yet begun to fall. As tie did so a figure suddenly dashed into the room, then turned round, closed and fastened the window, and dropped the curtain over it. "Hallo I " cried Horace at this abrupt intrusion. 'Thit the man who had thus unexpectedly effected an entrance did not seem to heed him ; with a by cry of satisfaction he crossed the apartment and crouched over the blazing fire. 1 "I say, my friend, you make yourself at home I here," cried Horace, startled for the moment, but reassured the next, as he contrasted the slight and meagre form before him with his own stalwart proportions. "I could chuck the fellow out with one hand if he means mischief," Horace thought to himself.

The figure turned and lifted a haggard face of entreaty. "Save me," it said in low, hoarse tones. "Save you P Oh !" as Horace noted the visitor's dress, with the "broad arrows" stamped upon it, "I see ;.you've broken out of Princetown, eh ? " _

The Viiitor nodded. "Yesterday," the man said in the same choked voice, "and—l've been out on the moor ever since. They're after me now. Hide me! Save me!" And he crouched over the fire again. The miserable aspect of the creature before him aroused Horace's instincts as a professional healer. "Here," he said, pouring•out a cup of hot coffee (the remains of his own meal were upon the table), "drink this, and eat a bit of bread, and then you'll be better able to speak." _

It was pitiable to see the wolfish hunger with which the man despatched the provisions. But the food and warmth brought some colour to his wasted cheeks, and his voice was calmer and firmer as he said, gratefully: "Thank you, sir. I was almost dead when I came in. But better be dead than in that place yonder." • _

"Well, you'll have to go back there, you know," said Horace, hardening his heart with a sense of duty.

The man wrung his wasted hands with a gesture of despair. "No, no, no!" he cried. "Oh, sir, take pity upon me! lam an innocent man!" "I never knew of the convict who wasn't, upon his own shewing," said Horace. "I am innocent of the crime I was accused of," said the man ; and there was a truthful ring in his voice. "But I suppose no one will believe this as the jury didn't," and he bent again over the fire. Horace looked at his visitor critically. He was a young man of about twenty-five, certainly without the "criminal expression stamped on so many countenances among the convicts. He had an honest, open face, and refined features, and yet— well, appearances are often deceitful. ' The sound of wheels was heard outside.

"Don't—don't give me up !" cried the man, catching at Horace's hand in an attitude of despair. And the young doctor's heart was not proof against the piteous appeal. "I'm a fool and worse," he said to himself: "but, anyway, I can take time to think the matter over. Look here," he added aloud, turning to his visitor, "I daresay you're lying to me, and any- way, I've no right to shelter a convict from justice. But if you like to remain here until I come bac: —l'm called out to see a sick person—well, you may do so." "But if they track me here? " cried the man. _

„ Horace hesitated a moment, then walked to the wall by the fireplace and pressed upon an ornament in the ancient oak-panelled wainscotting which lined the apartment. A panel sprang open and showed a deep recess behind. Horace had recently accidentally discovered this concealed cupboard, and had not yet mentioned it even to his old housekeeper. "If you hear anyone coming, you can get in there,” he said. "The door will shut if you pull it, and there are ventilation holes above. But remember," and Horace strove to speak sternly, "I'm not going to have a hand in compounding a felony. You'll have to give yourself up when I come back; at least--" But at that moment the gig dashed up to the door, and Horace's visitor vanished into the cup- board just as old Ifartha, the young doctor's house- keeper and factotum, entered to say that "Roger was awaiting." As he drove rapidly away the young doctor reviewed his adventure. It went against his kindly instincts to deliver up the wretched man, and yet , duty must be done. "The poor wretch looked half dead. It won't hart to let him have a warm and a sleep," reflected the young doctor. "And if he gives himself up, as I must see that he does to-morrow of course, maybe it will go easier with him than if he was caught by the warders." And then Horace began to think of the patient whom he was going to see. Halting at length at one of those lonely farm- steads which dot Dartmoor here and'there, Horace was ushered into a bedroom where lay a young woman, who had been a pretty girl once before she grew so pale and thin. Horace had been attending her for some weeks, and was much interested in the case. Without any organic disease the girl was certainly wasting away; and the conviction that some mental trouble was at the root of her illness was growing stronger in Horace's mind every time he visited her. This evening he spoke out openly upon the subject. "Had your daughter received any shock? Has she any trouble upon her mind ? " he asked as he quitted the sick-room. Mrs. Fry, the comely buxom farmer's wife, angered her apronstrings for a moment. "I think—•maybe—it was them guns a-going which set Ellen off so bad \ with the spasms to-day," she said. "What guns? Oh, ytm mean the ones fired front Princetown to give notice that a convict had I heard them „myself ; but why should

"Folks did say afterwards as Charlie had been a fool not to have engaged a sharper lawyer, as the other man did; but Charlie, he seemed so certain sure—being quite innocent, as he said he was—that the truth would come out at the trial—that he didn't particular trouble about his defence." "Do you think the young man was guilty?" asked Horace.

Mrs. Fry again fell back upon her apronstrings. "Well, sir, I hardly know what to say. The jury thought he was, any way, and they ought to ha' known. But there was one person "—and here a note of grievance stole into the good woman's voice—"as I don't believe judge, nor jury, nor anyone in all England, would ha' made think him guilty, and that was Ellen. Clean contrary the girl was, as her father said." "Well, she would naturally believe in her lover," said Horace. _

"It's an unlucky business that he ever teas her lover," said Mrs. Fry, in the same injured tone. "Many and many a good match that girl might ha' made, and to come to this at last! For, as her father says, whether Charlie did it or didn't, 'tis much the same now; he's got his five years' pens' servitude, and ain't a fit husband for any respectable girl. But, if you'll believe it, sir, Ellen vows she'll marry him as soon as he comes out of his time; and I'm afraid she'll do it, too, for she's obstinate—like her father--when she takes an idea in her head, for all she looks so quiet. Why," and hero Mrs. Fry cautiously lowered her voice, "father don't know it, and I hope and pray he never may, for it's fine and angry he'd be if he did, but since Charlie was brought to Princetown, Ellen has been to see him —she wheedled round Jim--that's her brother, ghfhooee fool lsoatroeesfnri gythhoetearc of lieli nd der: do—wt o—t Charlie,lwn 01E2. nn, one tldievi nei ibrn edg madeiad aae yl 'twassand Jima t f sheatahk eer seeing him so dreadful altered in his looks (Jim said he wouldn't ha' known him) that's made Ellen as bad as she is." It was a sad, pathetic little story. Horace began to wonder if he held any clue to it; he drove home deep in thought. "And it's glad enough I am to see you back, sir," cried old Martha, who was watching at the door. "There've been a pretty set-out since you was gone, and I declare I'm all of a shake still. You'd scarce drove off before a couple of men with guns, prison warders they said they was, knocked at the door and said they was after a convict, as they believed they'd seen cut in through you" window. I said you was out, but I begged 'em trn mercy's sake to come in and look all over the house, I being a lone woman in it, you and Roger both away. The men was very civil, and they looked well everywhere—l will say that for them— under the beds and into the cupboards; for, as I said : 'Oh, gentlemen, whatever will become of me if there's one of them savages lurking about the house ? "Did they find anyone ?" asked Horace. "No, sir, they said they supposed, if the man had slipped in unbeknown, he had just slipped out again; I told 'em you'd only just gone out, and, of course, the man wouldn't have stayed to face you, knowing as you'd give him up quick enough. - Did Horace's conscience give him an uneasy twinge? "Well, I needn't keep you up any longer, Martha," said the young man, entering the parlour, where a comfortable supper meal was spread, and the old woman trotted away to bed. Horace turned the key in the door, then softly opened the cupboard. His visitor was lying along the floor inside, so still and quiet that for the moment the thought crossed Horace's mind that be was dead, but as the young doctor hastily put his hand upon the man's heart, he stirred and I murmured "Ellen!" then opened his eyes and I feebly tried to move. Horace assisted him to a chair,mixed him a small quantity of spirits and hot water, and supplied him with food. "Sir, you have saved me," said the man, grate- fully, as he looked round; "and now—after all your goodness—you won't give rue tip?" "You are better now, and able to talk; tell nre your whole story—the truth, mind, and no lies,' said Horace. As the young doctor expected, the narrative that followed was the same history which he had recently heard from Mrs. Fry. The young man related his story simply and straightforwardly, and, as Horace listened, a conviction of the speaker's truthfulness grew upon him. "But the evidence was very strong against you," he said.

"I know it was, air, so strong that I can't blame the jury for deciding as they did," said the young man, quietly; "but, all the same, I am as innocent of the crime as you are. 'Twas Bates was the thief, only I couldn't prove it." Horace was silent; he knew well the reputation of the lawyer to whom the other clerk had entrusted his defence—a very shrewd and not too scrupulous criminal advocate. The only way to clear his own client was to fix the guilt upon Lovell, and this appeared to have been success- fully done. _

It was perhaps fortunate for the game of conceal- ment which Dr. Thorold was playing, that his faithful housekeeper developed a severe cold which wiae,L3i.. *. 'keen her 'Dec/ tar a ;Val PAPA.

The meat and bread aione that was eat in them few days—why, there might have been a hungry man in the house to keep." Possibly there was! Dr. Thorold was by no means easy in his own mind regarding the course he was pursuing. Though convinced that, in Lovell's case, there had been a miscarriage of justice, in the eye of the law the young man was a convicted criminal; and it is not the part of a respectable and law-abiding citizen to assist "prison-breakers." The young doctor was perfectly aware that he was laying himself open to serious pains and penalties by acting as he had done, and passed a sleepless night himself after his uninvited guest had been safely ensconced in the laboratory. To keep Idpvell here indefinitely was impossible. After much cogitation Dr. Thorold decided upon taking the young man's sweetheart into his confidence. "Woman's wit" might help him. "She be-just the same, doctor; she don't eat, and scarce do speak. and takes no interest like in anything," sighed Mrs. Fry at the doctor's next visit to her daughter. "And to think what a fine, lusty, cheerful young woman she did use to to. Poor girl t"

"She may get better yet. I will sit and chat wlth her for a little to-day and see if I can brighten hr up," said Dr. Thorold, glad to find that Mrs. Fey, seeing that the invalid would "have cora- piny," as she phrased it, for a little time, took the oportunity of disappearing to look after some h yusehold affairs.

Cautiously and gently the doctor told his story. I s effect upon the invalid was magical. The listless sufferer, whose interest in life seemed ended, was suddenly converted into the alert, eager woman, able and ready to risk all to save her beloved. The girl sat up suddenly in bed, clasped the doctor's hand, and carried it to her lips. "Oh, bless you, sir, bless you, for your kind- ness," she cried.

"I'm acting most illegally, you know," said Horace.

" Illegally ! To help an innocent man!" cried Ellen, with all a woman's fine disregard of logic. "Heaven will bless you for your goodness, sir." "And now I want to know what's to be done next F " said Horace.

Ellen lay wrapped in thought for a few minutes. "I'll speak to Jim. Jim has a clever head, and he believes in Charlie as I do. I'll settle some- thing with him, sir, and then tell you—" "No, please tell me nothing," said Horace, hastily. "I've known, and done, too much in the matter already." Ellen smiled brightly. The colour was already cnming back into her cheeks, and the dimples beginning to play round her mouth. "Very well, sir, we'll wait to tell you anything till we've got Charlie safely away." It was a great relief to Dr. Thorold, on entering the laboratory a few denings later, to find the window open, his prisoner disappeared, and a few lines of grateful farewell in an envelope addressed to himself upon the table. "Jim has a plan and has come for me, and will sae me safe across the moor to Plymouth," was all the information given in this epistle; but later on, Horace learnt the full history from Ellen. "Jim" had a friend who was captain of a small coasting vessel, upon which the runaway shipped as a "stow-away"; only the captain being c ignisaut of his presence. At one of the ports of oal Charlie Lovell was successfully passed into a steamer bound for Australia, where he arrived in due couese, and found employment, under another name, in a Melbourne store. The criminal was not o! sufficient importance to the authorities to make the police particularly on the alert for his capture. Horace did not forget his promise of laying Lovell's, case before his lawyer brother. "The young fellow was a victim to his fool of a selicitor, wrote the latter in reply. "His lawyer missed all the , strong points in his case, and of c mrse A— (the counsel for the fellow-clerk) was sharp enough to profit by this. But I think I see my way to obtain a piece of evidence which may brine home the guilt where it is due, and you may see Mr. Bates in the dock yet." And, indeed, Clement Thorold so vigorously pushed his investigations that this, result actually followe& within a few months after •Lovell's escape from prison. Once in custody, a host of ugly stories began to crop up against the accused clerk, who was, in fact, a scoundrel of the first water. He never stood his trial, however. He committed suicide in his cell shortly after his commitment, leaving behind him a iwritten confession which explained how • the web of suspicion had been woven around his unfortunate fellow-clerk.'

..-.-- - Nothing is done hurriedly in legal matters in this country, and.even the proving of the innocence of a condemned person takes time; but, in due course, Charlie Lovell was able to return to Eng- land having, in accordance wit@ the orthodox custom in such circumstances, received a "free pardon" for a crime which it was conclusively proved he had never committed. Lovell's former employers behaved generously. They made him a liberal compensation, and reinstated him in their employ in a superior position to the one he had formerly occupied. A. blithe wedding shortly took place. at the farm, at which Dr. Thorold attended as an honoured guest. "That's our doctor," said Mrs. Fry to one of the London visitors. "Clever t I should just think he was. You'd never believe, to see Ellen's rosy cheeks to-day, what a poor, die-away soul the dear girl was when Dr. Thorold came first to her. I never thought to have had her above ground with 114 now. If it hadn't a-been for doctor, there mightn't have been no bride here to-day, as I toile father." The good woman did not know that but for Dr. Thorold's good offices the bridegroom also might have been missing from the feast. Horace kept discreet silence regarding his kindly deeds, and the three sharers of his secret, the newly-wedded couple and Jim, were equally reticent. For the law gives an ugly name to such philan- thropic deeds as Horace—and Jim—had lately indulged in; and, though Lovell's innocence was now proved, he had been a convicted felon at the time Dr. Thorold concealed him. Long years afterwards, Sir Horace Thorold, the fsmous London physician, related this adventure of his youth to his grandchildren. "Though remember, ray dears," the old gentle- man had added, "though it all turned out well in the end, I am not at all sure that I did rightly at the time."

"Sir, I think you believe me, and you won't give me up," said Lovell, suddenly, lifting his eyes to Horace's face. "I am conxmitting an offence against the law if I don't," said Horace ; "but—well, I will own that I think there may have been a mistake in your case. It was a fatal blunder on your part not to have engaged better counseL " "1 thought, as I was innocent, it mould be sure to come out all right," said the young man, I simply. , "I have a brother in London who is a clever lawyer," said Horace; "I'll draw up an account of i ' your case to-morrow and send it to him. If we can I lay hold of any fresh evidence we might place it before the Home Secretary. MY DEAR CHILDREN, I am pleased to tell you that , received a great many letters telling me how you spent your Easter Holidays, but I fear I cannot publish results till next week, as I really have not had time to go carefully through all that I have received. I see that you take great interest in your gardens, and that you fre- quently water the plants. This is quite right, as we have had so little rain. , I want you all to write and propose a separate Oompetition for boys and girls. Tell me what you think would be nice for you all to do for a monthly competition for June. You will all remember that the essays on " Spring " must reach me by May 19th, and please remember that the neatness and good writing will be taken into consideration, as in all contributions. I am awarding a prize this week to Margaret Sarll, for her contribution, "Only a Girl." I think you will,,all find this very interesting. I find that many of you are still asking me for the date of my birthday, although I published this some time ago. However, I will tell you all once more that it is on the 26th of June, and I hope on that day to receive a letter from all ray nephews and nieces. ' I shall look forward to hearing your ideas for a competition, and shall adopt what I consider the best, and, of course, offer very nice prizes. I must say good-bye now, or I shall take up too much space. So with all good wishes, from UNcrx Nun.

WHERE THE GREAT RED OWL LIVED. (Concluded.) " Whoo Whoo I Whool" called the great red owl, sitting at the top of the long red ladder.

Then down the ladder came a huge, shagg. y, long-haired animal, and reared himself on his hind legs in front of Wilson. "I will be good! I will be good! Oh, Mr. Great Red Owl, I will be good!" Wilson cried. "Whool " said the great red owl. Then the huge, shaggy, long-haired thing put two great paws on Wilson's shoulders,. and his mouth close to his ear, and shouted in a great, rough voice: "Naughty Wilson! Naughty Wilson!" and then went clambering noisily up the ladder. "Whoo! Whoo! Whoo!" called the great red owl, sitting at the top of the long red ladder. "Whoo! Whoo! 'Whoo! Thou hast been named by the striped chipmunk, the red squirrel, and the shaggy bear, and I have marked thee with the mark of the great red owl. Naughty Wilson! Naughty Wilson! Wi.oc i Whoo 1 Whoo!"

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am writing you this letter, to tell you that I should like to become a mem- ber of the ," Children's Corner," and I am sending you this little piece of poetry, called

• MIND THE PAIL! Young Tom is such a playful oat, He makes his mother weep; Some mischief he is always at, Except when he's asleep. Although the staircase is so wide, That naughty Tom prefers, Instead of walking down, to slide Upon the bannisters. t•I think you'll say it served him right, When sliding from the top One day, to get a dreadful fright— He found he could not stop. • But that was not the worst of all. You should have heard him yell— A pail was standing in the hall, And right in it he fell. It cured him quite, so I've heard say, Although you'll never fail To make him very cross to-day, By saying, "Mind the pail!" —From MAnnu REDMAN.

AN EXPLANATION OF A CIRCLE. An inspector was once examining a clacs ot boys, and asked them what was a circle. After some different answens, one boy Put up his hand and said, "A circle is a round straight line, with a hole in the middle." Your loving Niece, • PRUDENCE MUSTY. '

Then a bright white light shone at the top of the long red ladder, and down came, walk- ing, stepping daintily on the rungs, a beautiful maiden, dressed .all in white. When she reached the bottom, she placed a white hand on Willson's forehead, and said in a voice, like the tinkling of silver bells, "Naughty Wilson. Look! look, naughty Wilson.!" And Wilson looked, and saw through the side of the great hollow tree, past the big red oak, the crooked white brook, and the huge black rock, into his own little bedroom, where a candle, burning dimly, showed his 'mother kneeling by the side of his empty bed, and his fath.ef standing by, with his hand resting softly on her bowed head. 1:14 could not see her face, but he knew she was sobbing. His father looked very sad, and there were tears in his eyes. "Oh, I will be good! I will be good! Take me home!" and Wilson fell sobbing at the feet of the beautiful maiden. The beautiful girl bent and kieeed him on the forehead, and 10l the side of the hollow tree opened, and they floated through the air, light as thistle down, and as swift as arrows, past the big red oak, the crooked white brook, and the huge black rock, to the door of his house. The door, flew open, and Wilson rushed into z his mother's arms.

ONLY A GIRL.

Harold was not in a good temper. He did wish his mother had invited a boy to spend the Easter holidays with him, instead of his cousin May. They were out together one morning, and she was trying hard to be friendly, but he only looked ciose, and scarcely answered when she spoke to him. Presently they saw in front of -hem an old man, carrying a basket. "Let us creep up behind him, and tip over what he is carrying," Harold said, his spirits rising at the thought of mischief. "Oh, Harold, I wish you would not," May eaid; "you will make him so angry." - "What a silly you are I " her cousin said. "You have not a bit of fun in you. lam tired of having only a girl to go about with." The team came into May's eyes at being spoken to so crossly, so she said no more. Then Harold carried out his trick. But 'when he saw the result he looked rather alarmed, for the basket was full of eggs, and there they lay, a smashed heap on the road. "You young rascal!" the old man cried. "I shall go straight to your father, and tell him what, you have clone." "What are a few eggs?" Harold said, trying to carry it off boldly. "I can get you some more.

"Oh, can you? Well, this is a sitting of eggs from Mrs. Tucker's prize fowls, and I have just paid 2s. 6d, for them. If you bring Ine another Bitting before evening, we will say no more about it, though you do not deserve to be let off so easily." He hobbled off, and the two children were left together. "How could I know eggs were in the liasket?" grumbled r old .

"Have you enough money to buy eome more?" May asked. "Is it likely?" he said, turning out his pockets, and producing two pennies. " I shall get into a row, but it is no use thinking about that."

The annual Meetings of the abcve were held at Gl- borne on May 2nd,and were attended by representatives from the Free Church Councils at Southport, Wigan, Warrington, St. Helen; Widnes, Ashton-in-Makerfield, Platt Bridge and Golborne. A business meeting was held in the afternoon under the presidency of the Rev. A. Scott, of Southport, President of the Federation. The Secretary, the Rev. Alex Harvey of Widnes, sub- mitted the annual report which showed that there were eleven councils in the Federation, allef which were in a healthy and vigorous condition and doing good work. Much .united work had been done along the lines of workhouse services,temperance and evangelistic work and in outdoor religious services. The report congratulated the Federation on the change in the parliamentary representation of the district. The Federation had in its area nine divisions, and in the late Parliament every one of these divisions returned a Member opposed to them on the Education question. But the General Election bad reduced the number to four, and the majority of the members were now in sympathy with them. For the great change Free Church,/ en could take no little credit to themselves.—The report was adopted and ordered to he printed. The balance sheet showed a balance at the bank of $l.l Os. 10d. The following ofScers wero then elected :—President Mr. Charles E.Parker (Penketh) ; vice-presients Rev. J. Chater and Rey, A. Scott (Southport), and Mr.T.Melling (Wigan) ;treasurer,Mr.R. A.Pilkington,J.P.(St.fieleas) ; secretary, Rev. Alex. Harvey (Widnes) ; with the following as the Executive :—Revs. Dr. Brook (South- (Warrington), F. H. 801 l (Widnes), J. ppor nt ) T. gye l'gesotn.Helens Messrs. T. Jenkinson (St. Helen F. R. Dawson Wigan), and J. Bridge (New- • ton). A public service followed the business session at which the Rev. Adam Scott preached. The delegates were entertained to tea by the local Council and in evening a well attended public meeting was held in the Congregational Church under the presidency of the newly elected President. The speakers included the folio wing:—Rev. J. Rbys Davies, Southport, Revs. F. R. Bell and Alex. Harvey, Widnes. A resolution approving the main principles of the Education Bill was carried unanimously but it was hoped the Bill would be so amended in committee that Training Colleces would also be brought under public control and all religious tests connocted therewith abolished.

TIDYING UP.

The average women starts to " tidy up " directly the rest of the household departs and usually becomes so absorbed in her occupation that, being alone, she will not trouble to prepare a light lunch for herself, but simply works straight on until it is time to get the meals ready for the homecoming of the family. She „tells herself she can wait and have dinner with the .others, and naturally is too " lone up " to enjoy it. It is necessary to take some form of light nourishment during the day, and according to Sir Francis Laking,the King's physician, Ctirrant Bread answers the purpose exactly. It is a perfect food, inexpensive, light, diges- tible, and contains all the elements which sustain and nourish the human body. Being so palatable Currant Bread is the very thing for the housewife to eat at odd moments, and so pievent exhaustion.

A MAD DOG LASSOED.

A New York mounted policeman, formerly a cowboy chased a mad dog along the city streets on Saturday (says the" Telegraph " correspondent). lassoing it with his lariat, hauling it half-strangled to the pommels of his saddle, and then shooting it in the head with a revolver. The dug had menaced several people, and before his capture bowled over several women and chil- dren. Such wild-western methods of capture naturally excited flinch curiosity, and the policeman was cheemd for his skill.

FOES TO MEMORY.

Scientists have discovered that the memory is stronger in summer than in winter. Among the worst foes of the memory arc too much food, too much physical exercise, and, strangely enough, too much education.

TEMPERATURE AT GREAT HEIGHTS.

Some interesting meteorological observa- tione by means of unmanned balloons are re- corded in the "Proceedings of the American Academy of Arts and Sciences." Some remark- ably low temperatures for the higher atmo- sphere are given. At a height of ten and a-half miles 62deg. below zero Fahrenheit was regis- tered at St. Louis in September. In November and December, at about ten miles 721eg. below zero was reached, and 76deg. at rather under seven miles. The lowest temperature recorded was at a height Of nine miles, in January, when the thermometer fell to Illdeg. below zero. The lowest previous record was probably that obtained by a balloon sent up from Paris in October, 1895. At a height of about nine miles it registered a temperature of 94deg. below zero.

The Examiner Saturday, May l2, 1906. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS. MUSIC IN THE PARK. Association of Bands Annual Meeting. The annual meeting of the Warrington Association of. . Bands was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday, Mr. A. Down presiding over a representative attendance. PRESIDENT'S ADDRESS. Mr. A. Dow; the President of the Associat ion I thanked the members for the honour they conferred upon him by electing him in May last,year to the position of President. The Association was formed on the 13th of May, 1892—almost exactly fourteen years ago—during the whole of which time, until his death, his father occupied the position of President, and he (Mr. Down) could assure them that the progress of the Association and of music generally in Warrington were to his father,matters of the liveliest interest,and nothing but increasing ill-health ever prevented him from being with them on occasions such as that. The Secretary's report for the year 1905 was again very satisfactory and showed that the Association was in a prosperous con- dition and was still fulfilling the useful purpose for which it was formed. " Unity is strength," and if they were to advance they must preserve and foster that unity. He did not mean merely unity of membership in the Association, but also unity of purpose, by all the means in their power, to promote amongst the members of the bands, careful private study and regular attend- ance at rehearsals, and so to give in the Park, the best possible performances. Amongst the large crowds which assembled in the Park to hear the bands, there most be many who never heard any other music, except that of the street organ, and whose musical impressions were largely formed from the bands they then heard. A fine performance must, therefore, not only give a great amount of pleasure to many who had little enjoyment otherwise, but, he firmly believed, it was also one of the principal factors in inducing young men and boys to take up the study of musical instruments, and so prepare them- selves to become the bandsmen of the future. Vacancies, from one cause or another, were constantly occurring in the bands, and the young men had to take the places of the old ones whose playing days were over. They had within the past month, lost by death two musicians. One of their,—Mr. Tryal Holcroft,—was a highly-valued member of his father's military band and also of the Borough band and several other bands in, or around, Warrington. The other—Mr. Wilkinson,—with whom he was personally acquainted, was also much esteemed by the band with which he was connected. With regard to the working of this Association, he was informed by the Secretary that there were not any adequate rules in existence, regulating the composition of the bands which occupied the stand. He did not wish to suggest any course which would hamper or impair the efficiency of any band for public I performances, but he thought that the matter was worthy of the careful consideration of the committee. With regard to deputies playing in bands, he thought that any rules which might be drawn up, should .be elastic enough to allow of all reasonable vacancies being filled. Apart from a player being unable to be present through his own indisposition or that of some member of his family or from some other legitimate cause such as a business engagement, it often happened that regular players could not be found for instruments which were an absolute necessity for a public perform- ance, and any rule prohibiting the inclusion of deputies in such cases would, he thought, be a hardship on the bands concerned. Changes in the membership of a band also temporarily weakened that band until the new players found their feet, and the assistance of one or two experieneed deputies was not only desirable but inevitable in such cases. There was another matter which he would like to commend to the consideration of the committee, and that was the advertisement of performances in the Park. In years gone by, it was customary to have hung in the Warrington omnibuses, cards bearing the words "Band in the Park to-night." By the kind permission of the Tramways Committee and by the courtesy of Mr. Telfer, he was, last year, enabled to insert in the trams somewhat similar notices, on the occasions of the appearance of his father's band in the Park and he thought that if arrangements could be made to do this in all cases, the performances would be still further popularized. In conclusion, be considered the Warrington Association of Bands had had a most useful and prosperous career, and trusted that continued prosperity would long attend it. (Applause.) SECRETARY'S REPORT. Mr. B. WADSWORTH presented his report in which he stated that through the season of 1903 the weather was favourable end the bands were able to give all the performances arranged. The Association consisted of ten bands—Mr. Down's, Volunteer, St. Pauls'.League of the Cross,Bine Coat School,Borough,Padgate Industrial, Crosfield's, Walker's and Legh-street,—and it was pro- bable that for the coming season the Salvation Army Band would be added to the list. Financially they commenced the year with an adverse balance of £1 4s. 80., and closed with a balance in hand of Li 3a., 10d. (Applause). The report was approved. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. The officers of the association, the Chief Constable, The Town Hall Parks and Baths Committee, and the Press were thanked fur their services during the past year. The vice-presidents, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., the Mayor, Mr. F. Monks, Mr. R. A. Naylor, and the mem- bers of the Town Hall, Parks and Bath Committee, were unanimously re-elected. On the motion of Mr. DOWN, seconded by Mr. PETEU- SON, Mr. B. Wadsworth was re-elected secretary. Mr. J. Fairhurst was re-appointed treasuret, and Mr. A. Down was heartily re-elected president of the Associa- tion. It was decided to commence the performances on the afternoon of Sunday, the 20th inst., when the Volunteer Band will give selections. On the following Thursday evening Mr. T. J. Down's Band ,will open the pro- gramme of week-night performances. CRICKET.

WHEN DO WE WEIGH MOST?

Does the human body weigh lighter or heavier after tho partaking of a good meal? This is a question that has been put by a correspondent. The answer is, of course, simple, for as a rule the weight of food ingested in a given time greatly exceeds the output during that time of waste products. Thus a man may consume for dinner 11b. or 21b. of food in, say, an hour. In that time his average loss by the excretion of waste' products would be about 6oz. from all sources, so that during the thne of the meal he would be adding to his weight by the weight of the dinner less the weight of the waste products excreted. The net gain in weight would, there- fore, be 10oz. if his dinner had originally weighed 11b., or 11b. 10oz. if his dinner had weighed 21b. But the waste products lost during the meal would -be entirely due to respirable products which amount to a,bout 50 per cent, of the whole output, so that the gain in the given time would be greater. ,

THE LIGHT OF THE FUTURE.

The results of a study of phosphorescence in animals by Professor Wlntosh seem to have an important bearing on the problem of the light of the future. The most striking thing about animal phosphorescence is the simplicity and economy of the mechanism by which it is produced. There is no waste of energy in the form of heat, or other useless emanations, all is converted into light. On the other hand, in the very best artificial light only a small percentage of the energy ex- pended in producing it appears as light. A care- ful study of

-------- ANIMAL PHOSPHORESCENCE, then, may possibly lead to great improvements in our present methods of lighting. Some interoßt- ing investigations as to the cheapest form of light carried out at the Allagheny Observatory indi- cate that man must learn of the fire-fly and glow- worm. This 'conclusion was the result of a care- ful spectroscopic study of the fire-fly's lamp, and a comparison of the same with sunlight. It was found that all the rays emitted are light rays. and that no energy is wasted as heat or other invisible radiations.

MAGNETIC EFFECTS OF LIGHTNING.

VOlcanic rocks frequently show zones of small areas, distinguished by strong magnetic polari- ties. These magnetic anomalies have been ascribed to the effects of lightning, but there were no cases known, so far, in which the respective specimens had been tested before and after lightning had actually struck. Two Italian scientists, however, mention such a real case. They were engaged in investigating the magne- tism of the rocks near Mount Etna. During a September night lightning fused the telephone wire of a house surrounded by a wall built of basaltic rock; this wall had been examined and found to be hardly magnetic at all. Tho earth wire of 2.2 millimetres diameter running along tho wall (without insulators) remained intact; next morning the stones proved strongly mag- netic at a• distance of 13 centimetres from the wire on both' sides, and the polarity indicated that the magnetising current had travelled up- ward. The lightning-conductor of a new house was also struck; the conductor, a copper wire of 8 millimetres in diameter, is held by porcelain insulators, the wire being from 9 to 20 centi- metres from the wall. The stone wall had also become magnetic on both sides of the conductor, in spite of the distance, and again the current direction appeared to have been upward.

AN " INHALATOPIUM."

The various diseases of the lungs which 11710 susceptible of treatment by inhalation are pro- vided for in a new medical institute which has been opened in New York, where are installed the vapor6ing appliances invented by Dr. Bull- ing, of Munich. By means of these devices, a writer in Chambers': Journal points out, the liquids to be vaporised aro blown by corns pressed air into such infinitesimally fine par- ticles that they are carried by the_act of breath- ing into the finest ramifications of the lungs, and are thus able to reach directly diseases situated far beyond tho range of the ordinary inhaling-appliances. In the medical institute called the Inhala- torium special rooms are set apart for the Igo of patients, who sit for half-an-hour at a time breathing art atmosphere charged with the vapour suite,' to their, special complaints. In another room are a number of decha, each fitted with a small and restricted vaporiser ac!apted for direct application to the mouth or ncstrils. The air-compressors are fitted in the basement, and arc arranged to draw carefully cleansed and filtered air. which, after being warmed by a special appliance, is fed to the various vaporisers throughout the building.

RACE - BREEDING.

Max Isioraatt. whose oi)inions are always in- teresting and 'often curious, has very decided notions on the snbject of the . influence of heredity on race-development. He considers that the essential thing in race-breeding is not so much the selection of particular individuals —every individual having probably latent quali- ties of the best kind—s,s the creating of favour- able conditions for the dsvelopment of the good qualities. Marry Hercules with Juno, and Apollo • with Venus. and put., them ..in slums. Their 'children will be stunted in growth, rickety, and consumptive. On the Other hand, take the miserable slum-dwellers out of their noxious surroundings, house, feed; clothe them well, give them plenty of light, air, and leisure, and their grandchildren. pethaps already their children, will reproduce the :typo of the fine, tall Saxons and Danes of whom they are the off- spring. Redeem the millions of their harrow- ing care, give them ,plenty of food' and rational hygienics, ttid , allow their natural Gympathies to work out their mittrimo;lial choice, and you will liavo done all that ie., likely to strengthen, embellish, and ennoble the race. In one word, to be largely- efilcient, it must be considered, not as a biological, but as an economic quest:on.

.E WORKS COMPETITION. --NIFICENT ENTRY. A meeting of the committee of the Warrington Works Competition was held on Monday evening at which it was announced that 95 teams had entered, whilst over 600 players were registered. Besides the gold and silver medals offered by the Warrington Club, a generous offer has been made by Ifessrs. J. W. Bibby and H. H. Evans, the Warrington captain presenting a gold medal for the best individual batting performance, whilst Mr. Evens will provide a similar medal for the best bowling performance. The competition will be opened this (Friday) evening (6.15 prompt), when Oreenall, Whitley's are opposed to Whitecross. During May the games will be confined to Friday night, but in June two matches a week—Tuesday and Friday—will be played. 'l;he qualification of a player is that he must have been employed by the firm from which his club takes its name before May Ist. It is propcsed to play two games on Walking Day afternoon. Association Football.

Sir,—ln further reply to " Full Back "I have to state that in replying to correspondence, my replies are framed on lines to suit myself and not the individual I reply to, and If it will assist him in furthering his scheme,my two previous letters under this heading may be taken as "examples of the domineering attitude of the League towards its members." When the Warrington and District Football Associa- tion is a public institution the public will be presented with accounts without a request from him.—Yours truly,

T. P. BRADSHAW.

A Foreign Shoe.

Sir,—l have received from Spain a quantity of alparagata shoes. These shoes cost a few pence per pair, are made of cloth'with hemp soles and have been much talked of in the Press of late. It struck me many of your readers might be interested, so I will gladly for- ward these samples, as far as they will go to any who would care to see them. lam informed these shoes are universally worn in Spain aed that barefooted people are rarely seen there. 3, Lunt-road, Bootle, Liverpool.

HELENA KENNEDY.

(Our correspondent also encloses a letter from her cousin Mr. J. 0. Donnell, a schoolmaster in Ireland, thanking her for some of the shoes and remarking that he remembers reading about the alparagata shoes in the 'St. Helen's Exfoniner," some years ago when he was teaching in St. Helens.]

Street Paving.

The Property-Owner's Point of 'View. Sir,— I was much interested to note the attitude of Alderman Tinnion and Councillor Stott re the new order for street paving. Councillor Stott considers it unfair that property- owners should object to their streets being paved, and trusted the Committee would not be deterred from this work. In order to reassure Councillor Stott the Alderman announces that there is another batch of streets as long and—happy vision—still another, and for himself he will pursue an evenhanded course of justice. Obviously, with iludibras, For all he says he has a reason, For all he does a word in season And ever ready is to quote, Authorities for what he wrote." Tam curious sir to, know the reason and authority ' for " even handed justice" in this latest order. In it there are streets completed ten years and streets half I made. Streets with curbs and without. Streets that I form the centre of a district and streets that lead only to green fields. Streets where curb and side-walk have been put in and the centre cindered and kept dry so that after a storm they are in a better condition than parts of Sankey-street and Knutsford-road. And in this same order there are streets that resemble inluld seas 1 and miniature lakes. In other words streets where they have done everything possible, except paving, and streets where nothing whatever has been done. Will the Alderman explain the " even-handed justice," of this order to the owners who have done all they I could? Bet if one is curious to know the alderman's idea of " even handed justice "they may surely be pardoned I for awaiting with interest Councillor Stott's conception of fairness. Those who have watched his strange career in the council can well believe his ire is roused by the spectacle of those bloated capitalists, • the property owners, asking for consideration as they see the sword of Damocles suspended. They can well believe that Councillor Stott's method of fairness is to spend other people's money and thrust burdens on other shoulders that he never touches with his little Roger. They could forgive his advocacy of a variety of crude, ill-digested and fantastic schemes and projects,. that prove his mental limitations and economic ignorance, for like Nello ; "he knows many things but knows them all badly," if he himself contributed bv even a single penny to the cost of these experiments. 'Let Councillor Stott explain, if he can, what difference the enormous increase of rates has made or can make to him ; and if be is sincerely anxious to be fair let him advise the Council to devise a system whereby the vast bulk of the house- holders are made to directly feel the cost of the local expenditure, and not as now : shrink the margin of the unfortunate property owner, so that he will be saved from this travesty of justice,where those who select the tune do not pay the piper and can " Compound for sius they are inclined to By damning those they have no mind to." I enclose my card and am, &c.. CIVIC REFORMER. •

Religious Education.

Sir,—The Government's Education Bill continues to receive the severe criticism which so important a measure deserves. Fair criticism cannot be objected too, nay it is very desirable, but much that is directed against it is certainly not upheld by calm and dis- passionate reasoning but is supported by extravagant language and a distorted and exaggerated view of the intentions of its author, It was generally admitted that the task imposed on :qr. Birrell of forming a Bill which should lay the bogey of religious dithoulty by meeting the views of the many sects was an impossible one and his efforts thou to this extent have realised expectations. Admitting thn im- possibility of satisfying the men of extreme views then it is to the moderate men of sweet reasonableness to whom we look with hope for a satisfactory workable solution of this vexed question, an end so apparently unattainable that we almost despair,and feel that noth- ing short of complete secularizing of the elementary schools will be effective however much we regret it. The assertion that the Government has no mandate from the country to deal with the Education question is surely a preversion of the facts. It was given a prominent position in the address of every candidate, questions relating to it were repeatedly submitted to them,msetings were held on the eve of the elections by at least the Roman and Anglican Churcjaes and manifestoes issued advising voters how to exercise their privilege, and we see daily how members are being called upon to exercise their votes in accordance with their promises. If this Government has no mandate to deal with the subject what justification can be offered for the Govern- ment which so radically disturbed the previous settle- ment by their Act of 1902, sweeping away popularly elected School Boards which were admittedly doing a noble work in the cause of education, substituting a system of doubtful control and of more than doubtful efficiency to deal with so important and complex sub- ject. It was felt and admitted on all sides that such a settlement , and under such circumstances as the Act of 1902,c0u1d not be final and the Bill now under discussion is the natural result and sequel to it. Voluntaryists who from selfish motives have for years past opposed the creation of School Boards on the plea of extrava- gance now find themselves confronted with a system even more extravagant which is more directly felt whilst not as popularly controlled. But for their policy several provided schools would no doubt have been doing a use- ful work in our midst instead of a number of schools many of which do not answer the requirements of the Board of Education, nor is it possible by reasonable expenditure to make them. Great credit is due to the Anglican Church for its work in the cause of elementary education by the provision of schools but surely this was the legitimate duty of a wealthy and richly endowed State Church in return for its privileges rather than the impossible task of struggling Nonconformity whose riches and strength consisted in the earnest religions zeal of its living members rather than in the wealthy endowments of its pious dead. Still efforts have been made by them in this direction which have been successfnl even against an ungenerous opposition. I _ .

The Roman Catholic Church deserve more than a pass- im,: recognition as their schools have been built by con- tributions generally representing the sacrifices of the very poor on behalf of their faith and their children. Voluntary schools however were generally established as nurseries for the Churches of the denomination to which they belonged rather than as means to the educa- tion of the child. and sadly too often the selection of the teaching staff was governed by other considerations rather than their aptitude as elementary school teachers. In three veers experience of the School Attendance Committee I have never known a parent originate this so-called religious difficulty. They have been practically indifferent to the class or denomination of the school provided it was within reasonable distance of the parental home, and to my own knowledge children of Protestant parents have been educated in the Roman Catholic school who in later years have become earnest and usefnl members of the Anglican Church. If the Clergy, the Priest or the Parson, their personality, influence and opinions could be eliminated from the discussion pf this question the religious difficulty would disappear. The'gentlemen jealous of each other and of their privileges, judging by their correspondence are not overburdened with Christian charity, seldom in practical sympathy with the P ft or t s to solve those social problems on which depends the happi- ness and comfort of the masses, yet are generally keenly alive to their individual and collective interests. As in most rules there are exceptions, so in this, but these noble and self sacrificing men only prove the rule. We can agree with the Bishop of Durham—" The principles of the Education Bill are sound and right, in detail there may be difficulties and desirable alterations; the worst thing about it is the imtemperate language of the Clergy " With these men the joy of battle is one thing the interests of the children and the school another. They have no disposition to look upon the advantages of the Bill, but take every opportunity to enlarge upon details which seem to curtail their privi- leges. The main principles of the Bill, i.e., popular control and the abolition of religious tests for teachers are vital to the Rill and opposition to these by the churches will I am sure be a great mistake. Farseeing Churchmen recopalsed that when the Voluntary schools accepted rate aid the demand for complete popular

control would naturally follow and also that teachers as public servants should be freed entirely from sectarian control:ft is a measure of simple justice to the Noncon- formist teacher that there should be a recognized equality and that they should not be denied access to the 12,000 or 15,000 rate aided schools hitherto closed to them because their consciences were not sufficiently elastic to accept a religious test in which they dis- believe. •

Surely the advantages offered by the Bill are not to be despised by the Churches. It provides for religious teaching in every school. It allows definite religious teaching in Church schools two days a week. It leaves the Church in lull possession of her school, keeps them in repair for her and pays about .f. 500.000 annual rent for use of same. And yet the Bill is described as an Act of confiscation, spoliation and robbery by men who ought to know better to audiences who have not tired' or disposition to form an opinion for themselves. All this strife and bitterness is on the part of those who put the Church before the child, to secure or otherwise the privilege of teaching creeds and dogmas to the children of from four to four- teen years of age in attendance at our elementary schools,practically the children of the labouring classes. Are we then to suppose that the thousands of children in attendance and being taught in otherthan elementary schools which recognise no dogmatic teaching, are all going to the bad ? If not, why ? Is their saving grace the social status of the parents? To the sugges- tion that religious teaching is the work of the Church and more particularly the Sunday school, a dignitary of the Church replies:"We should never get the attendance" Why not? Is it because of the indifference of the parents, those who are represented as crying out for the dogmatic teaching in our elementary schools? If so, then it is very undesirable that the coercive measures of the Education Committee should be employed to compel them to accept a teaching to which they are so indifferent by including it in the schools' curri- cula. As the discusssion has proceeded we realise how the sects detest each other's religion and how alarmed lest their own truths should be taught by one outside their communion and that the Bible is so great a terror that some clerics would ban its use in the elementary schools altogether unless accompanied by their special dogmatic teaching. Bible teaching as adopted by the L.C.C., Liverpool and other school boards has been described as a milk and water diet without the milk. Well it is upon this teaching more than half the children of the coun- try have been nurtured many years and which has been eulogised by such men as the Present Archbishop of Canterbury, Dr. Tait, Cardinal Manning S:c., and of which Dr. Ryle when Bishop of Liverpool said : "I thank God for the Board school of Liverpool, I wish them all prosperity. Had it not been for them a large number of children now respectable members of society would have become a danger to the community." Whilst Ido not demand secular education I do not fear it with the Headmaster of Nottingham High School ".1 fail to conceive of any school where conduct is not an essential subject of instruction where children secure training in citizenship, neighbourliness,pnrity, honesty, kindness and the like. Nor do I believe that a school- master is devoid of any principles of citizenship and morals except those which are "systematically scheduled" in a" legal curriculum." The settlement must be on national not local lines. We must not for- get that more than half the present cost of Education is contributed by the National Exchequer ; the lesser half by local rates. The nation has generously accepted the principle that the education of a child in a broad sense shall be the duty and care of the State. Succes- sive Governments may make active or passive resisters, but the " new factor in British politics " who has gained his valuable experience in the turmoil of every- day life in his struggle for existence knows the exact value of religious education as applied in the factory, workshop, or in commercial life, and ultimately he will settle this as he will many other questions by the appli- cation of common-sense principles. GEO. WALKER. Springfield, Padgate, Warrington, May Bth, 1906.

TRADES COUNCIL AND MR. CROSFIELD, M.P.

THE TRADES DISPUTES BILL.

At a meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday evening, the Secretary (Councillor F. Stott) reported that in accordance with instructions given at the last meeting he addressed the following letter to Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P. :—At a meeting of the above Council held yesterday a resolution was unanimously adopted as follows : 'That I be instructed to write asking if you will kindly inform us why you did sot vote for the Trades Disputes Bill which was introduced by Mr. Hudson, MP., on Friday, March I 30th." Thanking you in anticipation of an early reply, I I remain yours faithfully, 14 RED STorr. On April 21st, continued Councillor Stott, he received the following telegram from Athens : "Councillor Stott. ' Warrington. tour frank and polite inquiry received. Replying by to-night's mail. Crosfield.' On April 20th he received a letter which he would read to the Council Councillor Stott then read the letter (a copy of which appeared in a recent issue of the "Examiner") in which Mr. Crosfield explained that he "paired "on the occa- sion of the introduction of tka Trades Disputes Bill. ' He (Councillor Stott) did not reply to the letter because he thought the members of the Council would probably instruct him in the matter.

The Chairman (Mr. Ptuain) said that however some people might think Mr. Crosfield had shirked his respon- sibility with respect to his Parliamentary duties,he had certainly not shirked their letter. . Mr. GREALET said he was not satisfied with Mr. Crosfield who had disappointed him. They did not send the member to Westminster to pair with some- body else. They sent him there to look after their interests, and two of the most important questions on which Mr. Brostleld was elected were Chinese labour , and trades disputes. He (Mr. Grealey) looked upon Mr. Crosfield at one time as being almost equal to a Labour I candidate and the working classes stuck to him and I returned him by an unprecedented majority. He thought Mr. Crosfield had acted dishonourably to the working classes of Warrington in not voting on the Trades Disputes Bill. He was committed ou Chinese I labour and the trades disputes question and he had ignored both, He (Mr. Grealey) thought the Council ought to censure the member for his neglect and insist on him being in his place in the House. Never mind the ancient games at Athens ! They did not want them but social reforms in England. One member who had not time to fulfil Parliamentary duties had resigned like a man ought to do, and if Mr. Crosfield could not fulfil his duties as member for Warrington he ought to be called upon by that Council to resign and let them I elect a man of their own. Mr. Grealey concluded by ' movjng : "That this Council censures Mr. Crosfield for his absence from the House of Commons during the discussion on the the Trades Disputes Bill." He as a member of the Progressive Labour League was not ashamed of Mr. Cro,sdeld and if he had been present when the letter was proposed at the last meeting he should have explained. He I happened to know who Mr. Crosfield had paired with, but he would not enlighten them now. He would move an amendment that they did not pass a vote of censure. The SEE'IiFiTARV said he did not think the amendment was necessary as those who did not, agree with the resolution could vote against it. He was not going to support the resolution for many reasons. He did not think they ought to pass a vote of censure. Mr. SMITH: No. It is a shame. The SF.CIIETARX: No it is not a shame at all; but Mr. Crosfield does not represent this Council. If he were a labour man financed by the Labour movement we should see that he was in his place Continuinr, he said they must not mind what the Pro- gressive abour, League said. Mr. Crosfield ought to have been satisfied to have answered simply the question put t 3 him. it was not fair to the political sections who were affiliated with the Council to stiffer an insult like that. They had men belonging to that Council who called themselves Conservatives—they had just as much right to be Con- servatives as Liberals—and Mr. • Crosfield ought not to use a question from that Council to insult the Conservative party, to make an attack on it, and to try I and show what a valuable Government we had at the present time.
