Bac. ably presided at the organ at all the services. The collections realized the handsome sum of 230. Are Socialists Atheists. The Baptist Church, Golborne-street was crowded on Sunday evening., when the Rev. H. Dunnico preached upon the subject "Are Socialists Atheists?" The question, he said, was one of supreme importance, because the Socialist body was a growing power, in every civilized country throughout the world. If , Socialism, therefore, was synonymous with atheism, I and hostile to the Christian Faith, then those who believed that the key to all social questions was to be found in the Gospel of Jesus Christ could not do other than do their utmost to kill and crush the movement las detrimental to the highest welfare:of the world. The ! question they had to answer then was this : Are we justified in terming—as some people did term— I Socialists atheists? His answer was an emphatic "No." Such a charge was baseless and with- out foundation. There was a thing termed : Socialism which was both a crime and a curse, ; just as there were systems of religion which were vile ' and loathsome; but just as religion could be essentially Christian so could Socialism. An atheist could be a a Socialist, a Christian could be a Socialist, but neither their atheism nor Christiauity was the product of Socialism. Just as a man could accept Darwin's teach- ing in science without accepting his views on Christianity, so could a man accept Robert Blatchford's teaching in sociology without joining him in a tirade against the Christian religion. Socialism pure and simple, was an economic doctrine, the soundness of I which might or might not be questioned. It was unjust and untrue to denounce Socialism as atheism. It might be true that some people were Socialist because of selfish motives, but some people were professed Christians for the same reason. Some Socialist might shout " down everything that's up," but that was an explosive form of a principle good in itself. Christian people in their zeal and bigotry had carried out things equally ; unjustly. In conclusion, the preacher said that whilst , Socialism would not in itself make men angels, social injustice did make men devils. ! Bishop;of Liverpool and Sunday School Instruction. • Sermon at Warrington. Special services on behalf of the Sunday school were held at Holy Trinity church on Sunday. In the morn- ing the Bishop of Liverpool preached, taking for his text" He saith unto me, Feed My lambs.' " He said those words formed the great Education Act of the Church of Christ, an act passed not by a Parliament of I fallible men, but by the Divine Head Himself, for all centuries, and for every nation under heaven. He was there to speak on behalf of the Sunday school, and he would like to remind them of the place the Sunday school held in the Church's system, and how it was endeavouring to carry out the command of our Lord. The Sunday school could never take the place of the day school, the home, or the church. When Robert Raikes in 1731, moved by the ignorance, squalor, and godlessness of the children of Gloucester, opened the first Sunday school, he taught within its walls not only the Bible, but, quite I rightly, reading, writing, and arithmetic for those poor ; children who could neither read nor write ; but, for all i that, it was a school of religious cult, because they were tau gh t to read the Word of God. When at last the Church of Christ in this land—both the Church of England and the Nonconformists—awoke to a sense of their respon- sibility and founded the two great educational societies, the National Society and the British and Foreign Society, this secular work of the Sunday school was gradually transferred to the day schools, but the 'day schools were also used for religions instruction. It was constantly said in the present educational trouble that if secular education became the law of the land in our elementary schools,the Church could fall hick on her own Sunday schools. He ventured to emphatically. repeat that, good as the instruction might be in the Sammy schools, it could never tako the place of the day schools for three reasons—firstly, that the time given for the religious lesson, which would not be more than thirty-five minutes, on a subject so profoundly vital and pervading as religion, could not be taught adequately in one lesson lasting thirty-ilve minutes once a week; secondly, devoted, earnest, and able as many of their teachers might be, the fact that some of them had not been tl a ined to teach anti to give that sole religious instruction in school to the children might, well flit them with dismay. They might be doing their best, but they could not accomplish the impossible. Finally, if secular reducation prevailed in the day 'schools, and the Sunday schools alone gave religious Instruction, then a large number of the children who needed it most would never receive it. In the afternoon the Vicar (Rev. E. C. B. Carleton) preached and at the evening service the pulpit was occupied by the Hon. the Rev. C. F. Cross, Vicar of Stratton.

WHERE WERE THE DIFFICTITIES:?

They were not with the Free Churchmen. He ventured to think with what delight they would bold spiritual fellowship with the men who were going to speak, politically so'opposed to them, on Friday night. They would have no difficulty in worshipping with either one or the other. Somehow there remained a great difficulty on the Angican side. The Church could never be one because they were unable to allow—perhaps it was inherent in an Established Church—that breadth of opinion, and yet in spite of all their laws they had wider varieties of opinion within their fold than there were amongst the Free Churches outside it. Continuing Dr. Brook said he disliked the idea of Free Churches being taken up too much with pa:ty politics, but he recognized that they could not and ought not to endeavour to entirely divorce Christianity from politics. (Hear, hear). It could not be done any more than they wanted to divorce it from any other depart- ment of life. Dr. Brook went onto remind the audience of the great influence Christianity had had on public life at all times and showed what a part it had played in epoch-making movements. The rev. gentleman pro- ceeded to emphasise the functions of the churches.lt was their business to;keep open the channel of infinite good so that there might ever be an inexhaustible supply of energy to maintain the cause of reform. It was also their business to keep certain ideals before the minds of the people. The great ideal of the brotherhood of man was the principal of these. Even in England people often spoke of the lower orders as though there was no universal kinship. Snobbery was in fact so universal that it seemed almost as though it would be the last devil to be driven out of the world. Another high ideal was the dignity of the individual. Recent events in South Africa—the treatment of the Chinese— made him wonder whether the attitude of the British people had changed, but they had found that there was the deep-rooted feeling that no slavery should exist on British soil. (Applause.) It seemed sometimes as I though they were being deflected from their great ideals, that they were inclined to worship money, but deep down in the hearts of Englishmen was the belief that one must not be a plutocrat to be noble but that the masses of the people should be ennobled and made the children of God, and they could not have that sense and be true to it without ultimately revolutionising the world. (Applause.) 1 DR. TOWNSEND AND BIBLE 'LEACHING.

The Rev. Dr. TowNsTsNo said he must cordially agree with the speech of their Chairman. He was sure they were all better for having had the truth put before them as it had been in a spirit of such moderation and charity, and he trusted that spirit would characterise them throughout the great controversy in which they were engaged, and until the victory was won for justice and righteousness. (hear, hear.) He was deeplysorry that they had to meet in a controversial sense. It was not, however, of their seeking. It had not been of their seeking from the very first. As Free Churchmen they had never sought a battle in their history. Every fight they had had for 400 years had been forced upon them by the arrogance and assumption of those who opposed them, and they had been obligod to stand out in defence of the rights of Christian citizenship. (Applause.) They had the old spirit in:them'still find would never sheathe their sword until they had planted the New Jerusalem in this bright and sunny land. (Applause.) They as Free Churchmen on that question were not party politicians. Christianity in politics meant the uplifting of the citizen and the purification of the country, and therefore everyone who loved Christianity and righteousness should be possessed of a spirit of citizenship. It was not as party politicians they fought that battle, hut if there was one political party in the country which honoured THE DRINK INTEREST which would endow the drink interest and would turn an ' annual licensa into a freehold tenure, and if there was s party which would grapple with that evil and try to destroy it as a Christian man and a lover of his country he would take his stand with that political party and would fight to the death the other side. (Applause.) On the other hand if the other political party tavoured the drink traffic he would be found fighting it. He felt that in speaking mull fighting on that great' question he was doing as religious work as he ever did in his life. He did not fight the Conservative party as a Conservative party. He fought it as the party of .-exclu- siveness, reaction, sectarianism and injustice. He said they were bound by their principles as Free Churchmen and as standing for a Free Church and a Free Chris- tianity in a free land to drive out intolerance and exclu- siveness of every kind, and especially in regard to the children, who were the great russet of the nation. He would a thousand times over that the education question were settled, and that they could give over their whole strength to driving out the drink demon and struggling with the great questions of over-crowding, sweating and gambling, and the other evils and wrongs under which the nation was suffering and bleeding at every pore. Sectarianism during our whole history had interfered with the standard and prosperity of our education, and it was now too late for sectarianism to intrude again. (Applause). The speaker proceeded to express satisfaction at the aboli- tion of the dual system and religious tests for teachers and said the latter step necessarily excluded sectarian teaching at the public expense or in the public time or by public officers. If that were not so, there was no settlement of the question and they had years of con- troversy and bittereess before them. Mr. Chamberlain had appealed to the prejudices find bigotries of the House of Lords to deal hardly with the question when it came up. Let them try. (Applause). But they would not try. The House of Lords if it showed very little judgment and understanding in regard to the high- est questions in politics, it had strong instincts. (Laughter). He had an objection along with many others ,to the four-fifths clause. He thought that was a concession a great deal too liberal in the direction of sectarianism. At the same time he would sooner concede that than lose the Bill. They must strain their utmost energy to get the clause altered and amended in committee but he trusted that the Bill as it stood in fell its essential parts would pass into law before long and would prove the founda- tion of a system of education as high in quality and as noble in standard, arid Ks firmly established on the principles of equity and righteousness, as the whole world could show. (Applause.) Wkilst he rejoiced in the attitude taken by the Bishops of Hereford and Carlisle, Dean Stubbs, Canon Hensley Henson and others, the great mass of the Church of England had expressed a DEADLY OPPOSITION

Brotherhood to the trustees of the church, and the latter are to be warmly congratu- lated upon the acqutsition of so fine an instrument. Brfndley, giving absolute promptness of speech with perfect repetition. Its tone is characterised by great breadth in the foundation tone stops, while the softer and solo registers are remark- able for their beauty and individuality, and when the full organ is used, agreeable surprise is aroused at the musical effectiveness, produced by a comparatively small number of stops. SALE OF A MOTOR BICYCLE.

Sequel in Warrington County Court.

At the Warrington County Coart on Thursday, before his Honour, deputy judge Cuthbert Smith, Mr. George Venn, Sankey-street, sued Mr. W. J. Brown, electrical ' engineer, 3, Percy-street, for £lO, the price of a motor bicycle sold to the defendant. Mr. A. Browne appeared for the plaintiff, Mr. T. S. Steel representing the ' defendant. Plaintiff's case was that he had a motor bicycle for sale, and the defendant approached him with regard to the machine, and took it away to try it, Mr. Venn, agreeing, if the defendant was satisfied to sell the machine for £lO. Defendant returned a day or two later and said the machine had stopped about twenty miles away and would not go. Plaintiff examined the machine and found that it had stopped through want of lubrication. Thereupon the defendant took the machine away again and on the 2nd April telephoned to Mr. —Venn and said he was keeping it and ' would see him the following Thursday. Mr. Venn heard nothing from the defendant and the following i Monday sent nan account for the machine. The next day the bicycle was returned in a damaged condition. The crank had been broken and replaced by one of an entirely different pattern. The compres- sion was damaged and the number plate had been ' wrenched off. Later the same day the defendant telphoned and said he was returning the bicycle. Mr. Venn said the machine had arrived at his shop and that he took it under protest but refused to keep it as the purchase had been completed. On the 19th April he (Mr. Browne) wrote to the defendant, who a few days afterwards took the bicycle away again but returned it three days later, Mr. Venn being away at the time. The bicycle was in such a damaged con- dition that it was practically worthless. Plaintiff, cross-examined by Mr. Steel, admitted that on the sth April he wrote to the defendant asking whether he intended to keep the machine. Defendant contended that there was not an out and out sale, but that the machine was sold with an option of return. He tried the machine on the 21th March, when he went to Widnes. On the way back the machine stopped and he had to walk back. He oiled the machine at Widnes. On the 2nd April After another short trial, he smashed the cycle crank trying to start;the machine and sent it to the Mersey Cycle Company to be repared, giving them instruc- tions to return the machine to Mr. Venn. After the receipt of Mr. Browne's letter he went to Mr. Venn's shop and took the bicycle away to bare it valved. He was advised to refuse it and returned it to Mr. Venn. His Honour gave judgment far the plaintiff for the amount claimed with costs. His view of the transaction was that the defendant purchased the machine,and then, not being satisfied, tried to get out of it.

THE FREE CHURCHES.

ANNUAL MEETINGS OF THE WARRINGTON COUNCIL.

The Education Question.

PRESIDENT'S PRONOUNCEMENT.

The annual meetings in connection with the War- rington Free Church Council were held on Thursday and were highly suceassful. In the atternoon a well- attended service was held in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church and an eloquent -address, was delivered by the Rev. J. E. Roberts, M.A., 8.D., United College, Manchester, successor to Dr. McGaren, who based bis remarks on the texts, I. Corinthians xv 31, and 11. Corinthians iv 10. At the conclusisn of the service a tea was held in the Bold-street schoolroom to which a large number sat down. This was followed by a public meeting in the evening presided over by Mr.C. E. Parker presideat of the Warrington Free Church Council, and president of the South Lancashire Federation of Free Church Councils. He was supported by the Revs. D. Brook, M.A., D.C.L. of Southport, Dr. Townsend, Prestatyn, C. L. Stowe, J. F. Matthews, and H. Wall. [There was a large attendance and great enthusiasm I marked the proceedings. The Rev. J. F. Xlatthews lapologised for the absence of the Revs. S. Pitt and J. Manning, who were attending the Wesleyan synod at IPreston.

===== 0002601_19060512_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESIDENT'S ADDRESS.,

The PRESIDENT said : It is much to be 'regretted that so much of our time and thought at these meet- ings has, at present, to be taken up with the subject of national education. Not that the matter is of secondary importance, for the children are the nation's most precious asset, and it is of the utmost importance that we should have their education arranged on just and equitable lines. But I regret that th lecessary dis- cussion of this topic absorbs the th . and thought ' which we would like to give to other religious and social questions. (Rear, hear.) it is to be hoped that by the time we again meet in this capacity, an Act will be upon the Statute Book of this realm which will satisfy the sense of justice which is a characteristic of the English people. (Applause.) At the moment the air resounds with discussion in Parliamant,the Press,the pulpit, and the platform. We hear on all sides the clash of conflicting ideas. Unlike some I cannot see any- thing to regret in this. It is an evidence of the power and depth of our several convictions and of the variety of the human mind. (Hear, hear.) It is not the nren who, rightly or wrongly, think, feel, and speak strongly on these matters that are the. danger to national well- being and progress. It is the indifferent, the proverbial man in the street who is too. lazy to think of the prin- ciples involved, whose .mind does not 'grasp their importance, whose vision is narrow, who is for peace at any price, and who is like the drift-wood carried hither and thither by the strongest current that happens lc surround him. The discussion going on in this country is

EMINENTLY HEALTHY AND INVIGORATING and should not be deprecated. But to make this dis- cussion fruitful of good we must see to it, that what- ever side we take, we exercise moderation in language, soberness and clearness of judgment, accuracy in regard to facts, and above all, and through all, a Christian charity which will change the discord of conflict into an ultimate if far off, divine harmony. The centre of the present storm area is in the Anglican denomination, recognized by law, the Established Church of this country. I must ackpewledge that there are occasions when I have been disposed to be glad that there was an Established Church, and such occasions arise when as in times past the Church has nobly risen to the height of its responsibilities, expressed the national Christian feeling, and given voice to the conscience of Christian England. But recent utterances of some of the Church leaders will tend to make every Free Churchman who views with toleration the existence of an Established Church incline to the opinion that the Church has definitely retreated from the position of a National Protestant Church, the Church of the Reformation, and has gone back upon the teachings against which the Reformation was such an emphatic protest. (Hear, hear.) According to these utterances the authority of the Church ranks above that of the Bible, that the Bible should not be used unless interpreted by the Church. This tendency to magnify the Church has been recog- nised and protested aeamst by Churchmen as much as by Nonconformists. Tile Dean of Ripon said at one of the recent meetings of Convocation : " they found parents complaining that in the schools where clergy- men were teaching, the children were taught that the Church meant the Episcopal Church, a,nd in England it meant the Church of England in its narrower sense. The Bible is disparaged on all hands. The Church is put first and the Bible second. The children are told that the Church is to teach them, and the Bible is put in their hands as a proof of what the Church has said. Instead of teaching the little child the Psalms and the Sermon on the Mount, and bringing him in contact with God you are saying teach him the ideas that are the Church's teaching, and if he can get , hold of the Bible and verify it for himself, he may. The Bishop of Birmingham (long known as Canon Gore) says :' "to detach the Bible from the Creeds and the Sacraments of the Church and to treat it as self-explana- tory and capable of standing alone is to treat it in a totally unhistorical way, to treat it as it was never meant to be treated." Do you grasp the assumption contained in these words and their logical outcome? It is this, the Bible must not be taught unless it is accompanied by the Church catechism, creeds, and sacraments, and the high ideals of the Sermon on the fount must not he allowed to exercise their elevating influence unless under the organization and supervision of the Church. Happily we believe that these utter- ances many sections o? the Church.o n think the Church has ywree perbeeseelnat one be o f sorry so departed from its traditions, and histsry, as to DISPARAGE THE INFLUENCE OF THE BIBLE unless accompanieilby man-made creeds. (Applause.) many quotations might he given to show that many of the Church leaders have not departed from the belief that the truths of the Bible are accessible to all and that this simple teaching is powerful in its effect on the heart of man. Let one suffice. The Bishop of Manchester (who unhappily has spoken wild and in my opinion inaccurate words about the Education Bill) six Tears ego when Bishop of Coventry at a public meeting in Birmingham during a School Board election quoted with approval from a sermon of the late Head Master of King Edward's Grammar School and used these words: he confessed that the cry of the impossibility of undenominational teaching amazed him. There was no hook in the world which was so near-to the heart's com- pass as the Bible. Men who read the book di fTerently. but read it ' sensibly still find that was the effect it had upon them and that was the effect he desired to produce in the child,and whatever a teacher could do to enhance that effect without sectarian bias, they would give him liberty to de so." And yet strange to say this same Bishop is one of the most violent opponents of the present measure. On the other hand, Lord Hugh Cecil, who is to sneak to-morrow night in Warrington said at Bradford : " it was perfectly true that much of the teaching given in the past in Board schools had been, es many people declared, of great spiritual value, but it was also true , that that teaching was contrary to the idea of the ' Church, that it tended to undermine belief in Church- manship, and therefore was unfair to the Church of England." Think of it, a teaching which on Lord Hugh's own admission is of great spiritual value is to ' be jeopardised on the ground that it tends to undermine , Churchmanship. The

NA.RROWNESS OF VIEW and the escclusiveness implied is deplorable, and we can only say that if Bible teaching which is acknowledged to be of great spiritual value undennims the beliefs of the Church so much th 3 worse for those beliefs. (Applause.) In striking contrast ere the words of the Bishop of Carlisle, who says : " I will not sacrifice on the altar of my denominational doctrines,however sacred and sublime to rne,the spiritual hope of the nation and the religions training of hostsof children whose only opportunity of learning of God and their own divine origin and destiny is in the day school. To do other- wise is in my judgment not only a great national betrayal, but moral and soiritual infanticide in a stupendous scale." (Applause). It is much to be deplored that the Church of England should be so divided in regard to I3ibical teaching. Against the tendency—a tendency coming from various quarters,— towards secularizing the national schools the Estab- lished Church unhappily presents a divided and there- fore a weak front, a powerful section in that Church saying in effect if you do not have our creeds and our catechism in the schools you must not have the Bible. Surely as long as the establishment continues is it not reasonable to expect that it should be national in its sentiments and express and support in the national life those great fundamental Christian beliefs which alone can he called national? (Applause). The Church stands in great, DANGER OF LOSING. ITS NATIONAL CHARACTER, and if it elects to oppose the retention in our educa- tional system of the fmriamental truths of our national faith unless accomp.anied by the teaching of dogmas which half the nation do not accept, the time will be brought very near when it will forfeit all title to be callel the National Church. (Hear,hear), Let us then strenuously support the present education measure because it gives the surest guarantee for the training and disciplining of moral character and the building up of Christian citizenship, and achieves that end by pro- vision for the inculcation of moral truths, common to tho various sections of Christians, from the book which has been called with truth "the source of England's reatness." (Applause). FREE CEIURCHNIEN AND POLITICS.

The Rev. DAVID BROOK M.A., D.C.L., alluding to the education controversy said they were called upon to be magnanimous. It was their nature to be magnani- mous and they could not help being so in that particular

EDUCATION. ! ARCHDEACON SPOONER'S VIEW. " A National Peril." In the course of his first visitation charge at the, Warrington Parish Church on Wednesday, Archdeacon Spooner said theJ Education Bill, bearing the name of Mr. Birrell, but which, he thought, was probably very different from the one which he originally drafted, had introduced a grave religious crisis. They were con- fronted by a grave national peril, the possible outcome of the present controversy being a purely secular or , godless system of education. In view of that, as it ' appeared to him, it would be their wisdom to assume a firm but conciliatory attitude, and above all there should be a rapprochement between those who valued religion ' as the only reliable, because true, foundation of a national system of education, and Churchmen of all shades of religious opinion must present a united front, and speak as with one voice. It must be made a lay, not merely a clerical question, and parents must be aroused to the endangering of their God-given rights. , Teachers in their day schools, the majority of whom , Might be reckoned amongst the most devoted sons and daughters of the Church, must grasp the fact, and pro- test against the injustice of it, that their claims of con- science and liberty of action were being interfered with. The present administrators of the heritage of the past who held—and that for specific purposes—the proceeds of the generosity and religious feeling ot Churchmen of preceding generations, must realise that the sacred character of trusts was seriously imperilled. Last but not least, what about the children themselves ? Were they td be practically taught that religious instruction stood, in the estimation of the nation, on a level of unimportance as compared with all lessons ; that it was to be regarded as an "extra." not as vitally essential ? Were their eternal interests to be forgotten ? E 1 e believed that it was possible to bring about the secession from the ranks of the Nonconformists and apparent supporters of the bill of a large number of true-hearted men who still placed the vital claims of religion before that of political • partisanship. They must see to it that a healthy and earnest public opinion was at once fostered amongst all the religious-minded men and women of the nation and an emphatic and determined protest must be Uttered, not simply by Churchmen, but by all Christians. The present crisis needed to be grappled with by the laity even more than by the clergy.

fp) . 4fritt • • ,i40.411A 4.4 r; tycL IE3 If SSI P THE CLUBMAN. Sightseeing Awheel. Cyclists are sometimes blamed for not exhibiting greater curiosity in the inspection of local lions when travelling through unfamiliar districts, With- out being desirous of applauding those whose sole idea is the mileage to be covered, it must be pointed out that loitering is expensive of bodily energy. If a cyclist wishes to ride only fifty miles in a day, he must make his halts to view objects of interest • subsidiary to his cycling, or it will cause him some discomfort. On starting riding a cyclist gradually developes his full power as he warms to the work. Any prolonged pause causes a cooling down and a stiffening of the muscles. On resuming, a disin- clination for exertion is noticed. If frequent dis- mounts occur, quite a quantity of energy may be frittered away over a stage of ten miles, and a journey of any distance under these conditions is bereft of the pleasure usually associated with cycling. The Position of the Lamp. • _Those who prefer to carry a lamp on the fork •`6ll.e!kt of on the usual head bracket declare that rYf the Secure a brighter light immediately in front wheel. This would at first sight appear to *if , • 1 t„....= - be so, but from a study of these two diagrams, which have been made for me by an expert in the employ of one of the great cycling lamp manu- ' ; N facturing firms, it is evident that the advantage in this way is very slight. The sketches show the diffusion of the lamp rays in both positions. The drawbacks to having the lamp on the fork are obvious in stacking the machine with others in a stable or in a luggage van, when the lamp is • raorb liable to sustain damage in the low position. Although out of the rider's reach from the saddle for purposes of regulation, and more exposed to obscuring deposits of dust or mud on its glass, the fork-carried lamp is a necessity when the presence of a carrier obstructs the higher position. Patching Old Tyres. It is generally admitted that it is mistaken economy to pursue tyre repair to extreme. For example, when the lining of a cover has decayed, and the tyre has to be ridden soft to avoid a burst - from one of the many swellings on its time-worn tread, the time has arrived, not for the re-lining of -kt the cover, but for the purchase of a new one. • Every inch of patching canvas applied to the lining of a cover tends to slow and deaden the tyres running. The writer's experience of having tyres re-rubbered and re-lined has not been satisfactory, and, now that good covers can be bought at reason- able prices, the practice is not one to be recom- ruended. Whenever possible, tyre repairs should Itzle entrusted to the makers, it being to their interest ° keep satisfied the users of their particular tyre. Cycling and the Stage. Little Miss Iris Hawkins, who plays the part of the child in George Alexander's play, His House in Order,' at the St. James's Theatre, celebrated r, the hundredth performance of that successful pro- duction by having a series of portraits taken with her New Hudson bicycle. Many leading lights of the London stage still retain their devotion to the 'bicycle as the best means of keeping fit for their arduous professional duties.

The Closed Door. The increase in the number of motor cars, their speed, and the clouds of dust they raise, have proved too much for the residents in wayside cottages. No longer does the passing cyclist catch glimpses of domestic interiois across thresholds occupied by infants or household pets, for the doors in every hamlet are closed to keep out the plague of dust which afflicts the roadside dweller. Many and various are the preventiVes which are being tried by local authorities to mitigate the dust nuisance. Amongst them Akonia, an odourless preparation, applied two or three times a month by means of a watercart, is said to give excellent results. Electric Cycle Lamps. The application of electricity to the service ot the cyclist, in place of oil or acetylene gas, cannot be regarded as a practical success up to the present. The light afforded by the self-contained dry battery lamps is altogether inferior to that of an average oil-lamp, while the weight of an accumulator carried on the machine, and periodically recharged, compares unfavourally with that of a powerful gas lamp. About fourteen years ago, the writer experimented with an electric lamp, but was disappointed with the results. What the cyclist needs is not merely a light to denote his presence on the road to other traffic, but an illumination which shall enable him to command a fair view of his path ahead. .This advantage is possessed by the acetylene lamp, and in a lesser degree by good oil lamps, either of which render night riding safe and pleasant.
